# Chemistry — Answers

Questions: [questions.md](questions.md). Answer numbers match question numbers.

## VCE Units 1 & 2

### Unit 1 AOS 1: Elements, bonding and properties

#### Atomic structure and isotopes

**A1.** B. 20
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-1 -->

Explanation: Neutrons = mass number − atomic number = 37 − 17 = 20. 17 is the number of protons, and 37 is the total number of nucleons.

**A2.** D. number and arrangement of electrons.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-2 -->

Explanation: Isotopes have the same number of protons and therefore, as neutral atoms, the same electron configuration. Chemical behaviour depends on the electrons. Their neutron numbers and mass numbers are different.

**A3.** A. 26 protons, 30 neutrons and 23 electrons.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-3 -->

Explanation: Fe has atomic number 26, so it has 26 protons. Neutrons = 56 − 26 = 30. A 3+ charge means 3 electrons have been lost: 26 − 3 = 23. Gaining 3 electrons (29) would give a 3− ion.

**A4.** B. 63.6
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-4 -->

Explanation: Ar = (63.0 × 0.692) + (65.0 × 0.308) = 43.6 + 20.0 = 63.6. 64.0 is the simple average, which ignores that copper-63 is more abundant.

**A5.** C. an atom is mostly empty space with a small, dense, positively charged nucleus.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-5 -->

Explanation: Most particles passing through shows the atom is mostly empty space. Strong deflection of a few shows a tiny, dense, positive centre. Fixed energy levels come from Bohr's model, and the evenly spread charge describes Thomson's model, which this result disproved.

**A6.** Isotopes are atoms of the same element (same number of protons/atomic number) with different numbers of neutrons (different mass numbers); both isotopes have 6 electrons with the same configuration (2,4), and chemical reactions depend on electrons, so they react identically.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-6 -->

Explanation:

**A7.** Ar = (10.0 × 19.9 + 11.0 × 80.1) ÷ 100; = (199 + 881.1) ÷ 100 = 10.8.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-7 -->

Explanation:

**A8.** 16 protons means the element is sulfur; mass number = 16 + 18 = 34, symbol ³⁴S²⁻ (³⁴₁₆S²⁻); there are 2 more electrons than protons, so the charge is 2−.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ast-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Electron configuration and subshells

**A9.** D. 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s² 3p⁶ 4s¹
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-1 -->

Explanation: K has 19 electrons. The 4s subshell is lower in energy than 3d, so the 19th electron goes into 4s. A p subshell can hold at most 6 electrons, so 3p⁷ is impossible.

**A10.** C. 10
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-2 -->

Explanation: A d subshell has 5 orbitals, and each orbital holds 2 electrons, giving 10. An s subshell holds 2, a p subshell 6 and an f subshell 14.

**A11.** A. 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s² 3p⁶ 3d¹⁰ 4s¹
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-3 -->

Explanation: Copper adopts 3d¹⁰ 4s¹ because a full d subshell is more stable. 3d⁹ 4s² is the configuration predicted by the simple filling order, but it is not the observed ground state.

**A12.** B. [Ar] 3d⁵
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-4 -->

Explanation: Fe is [Ar] 3d⁶ 4s². When transition metals form ions, the 4s electrons are removed first, then the 3d electrons. Removing 2 electrons from 4s and 1 from 3d leaves [Ar] 3d⁵. [Ar] 3d³ 4s² would mean the 3d electrons were removed first.

**A13.** C. period 4, group 15 of the p-block.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-5 -->

Explanation: The 4p subshell places the element in period 4 and the p-block. The configuration ends 4s² 3d¹⁰ 4p³, so there are 5 valence electrons (4s² 4p³), which puts it in group 15. The element is arsenic.

**A14.** S: 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s² 3p⁴; S²⁻: 1s² 2s² 2p⁶ 3s² 3p⁶ (two electrons added to 3p, same configuration as argon).
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-6 -->

Explanation:

**A15.** Subshells fill in order of increasing energy, and the 4s subshell is lower in energy than 3d, so it fills first; each orbital holds a maximum of two electrons, which have opposite spins.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-7 -->

Explanation:

**A16.** Heat energy promotes (excites) electrons to higher energy levels; when the electrons return to lower energy levels they release the energy difference as light (photons) of specific energies/wavelengths, giving discrete lines; each element has a unique set of energy levels (different nuclear charge and electron arrangement), so the energy differences, and therefore the line pattern, are unique to that element.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ec-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Periodic table trends

**A17.** B. core charge increases while the number of occupied shells stays the same.
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-1 -->

Explanation: All period 3 atoms have three occupied shells. Core charge rises from +1 to +7, so the valence electrons are pulled more strongly towards the nucleus. The number of shells does not decrease, and neutrons have no effect on electron attraction.

**A18.** D. Argon
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-2 -->

Explanation: First ionisation energy generally increases across a period as core charge increases and atomic radius decreases. Argon, with a core charge of +8, holds its valence electrons most strongly. Sodium has the lowest value in the period.

**A19.** B. +6
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-3 -->

Explanation: Core charge = number of protons − number of inner-shell electrons = 16 − 10 = +6. +16 is the full nuclear charge, which ignores shielding by the 10 inner electrons.

**A20.** A. the valence electron is further from the nucleus, so it is lost more easily.
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-4 -->

Explanation: Both atoms have a core charge of +1. Potassium has an extra occupied shell, so its valence electron is further from the nucleus and less strongly attracted. It therefore has a lower ionisation energy and is lost more readily in reactions.

**A21.** C. be less reactive and have a lower electronegativity.
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-5 -->

Explanation: X is chlorine (2,8,7). It has the same core charge as fluorine, but its valence shell is further from the nucleus, so it attracts electrons less strongly. This makes it less electronegative and less reactive as an oxidising agent. Its radius is larger, and as a non-metal it tends to gain electrons, not lose them.

**A22.** Both ions have the same number of electrons in the same shells (isoelectronic); Na⁺ has 11 protons compared with 9 in F⁻, so its greater nuclear charge pulls the electrons closer, giving a smaller radius.
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-6 -->

Explanation:

**A23.** First ionisation energy decreases down the group; core charge stays constant at +1, but the number of occupied shells increases, so the valence electron is further from the nucleus; the weaker attraction (with more shielding) means less energy is needed to remove it.
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-7 -->

Explanation:

**A24.** Electronegativity increases across period 3 from Na to Cl; core charge increases and atomic radius decreases with the same number of shells, so the nucleus attracts bonding electrons more strongly.
<!-- id: chem12a1-pt-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Metallic bonding and properties of metals

**A25.** A. a lattice of positive ions surrounded by a sea of delocalised electrons.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-1 -->

Explanation: Metal atoms give up their valence electrons, which are delocalised throughout a lattice of cations. Bonding is the electrostatic attraction between the cations and the delocalised electrons. Alternating positive and negative ions describes an ionic lattice.

**A26.** C. layers of cations can slide over each other without breaking the non-directional metallic bonding.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-2 -->

Explanation: Metallic bonding is non-directional. When layers of cations shift, the delocalised electrons still hold them together, so the metal bends instead of shattering. Fixed, directional bonding would make the metal brittle.

**A27.** D. The magnetic behaviour of iron, cobalt and nickel
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-3 -->

Explanation: The sea-of-electrons model explains conductivity (mobile electrons) and ductility (non-directional bonding). It cannot explain why only some metals, such as Fe, Co and Ni, are magnetic. This is a known limitation of the model.

**A28.** B. softer and more ductile, with larger crystals.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-4 -->

Explanation: Slow cooling allows larger crystals (grains) to grow, so there are fewer grain boundaries and the metal is softer and more workable. Rapid cooling (quenching) produces small crystals and a hard, brittle metal.

**A29.** B. the delocalised electrons can move through the lattice when a potential difference is applied.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-5 -->

Explanation: The delocalised valence electrons are free to move and carry charge through the lattice. The cations stay in fixed positions in the solid, and metals do not contain free negative ions.

**A30.** Magnesium contributes two delocalised electrons per atom and forms Mg²⁺ ions, while sodium contributes one and forms Na⁺; the higher cation charge and greater density of delocalised electrons give stronger electrostatic attraction in magnesium, so more energy is needed to disrupt the lattice.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-6 -->

Explanation:

**A31.** The small carbon atoms occupy spaces (interstices) between the iron ions in the lattice; this distorts the lattice and makes it harder for layers of iron ions to slide past each other, so the alloy is harder and less malleable.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-7 -->

Explanation:

**A32.** Quenching (rapid cooling) produces very small crystals, giving a hard but brittle metal; tempering involves reheating the quenched metal to a moderate temperature and cooling it, which allows partial rearrangement of the crystals; tempered steel keeps much of its hardness but is tougher and less brittle.
<!-- id: chem12a1-mb-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Ionic bonding and ionic compounds

**A33.** C. Al₂(SO₄)₃
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-1 -->

Explanation: Al³⁺ and SO₄²⁻ combine in the ratio 2 : 3 to give zero overall charge: 2(+3) + 3(−2) = 0. Brackets are needed around the polyatomic ion.

**A34.** B. the blow shifts layers so that ions of like charge align and repel.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-2 -->

Explanation: A shift of one ion layer brings cations next to cations and anions next to anions. The resulting repulsion splits the crystal. Ionic compounds have no delocalised electrons, and the bonding between ions is ionic, not intermolecular.

**A35.** D. Magnesium oxide, MgO
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-3 -->

Explanation: MgO consists of Mg²⁺ and O²⁻ ions, which have doubled charges and small radii. This gives much stronger electrostatic attraction than in the 1+/1− compounds, and its melting point is about 2850 °C.

**A36.** B. transfers two electrons to an oxygen atom.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-4 -->

Explanation: Mg (2,8,2) loses its two valence electrons to form Mg²⁺. O (2,6) gains them to form O²⁻, and both ions then have stable noble gas configurations. Sharing electrons would be covalent bonding.

**A37.** B. the ions are free to move and carry charge.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-5 -->

Explanation: In the solid, the ions are fixed in the lattice. Melting frees the ions so they can move towards the electrodes and carry charge. Ionic compounds do not have delocalised electrons.

**A38.** Formula Ca₃N₂; each Ca atom loses 2 electrons to form Ca²⁺ and each N atom gains 3 electrons to form N³⁻; three Ca atoms transfer a total of 6 electrons to two N atoms, so the charges balance (3 × +2 = 2 × 3).
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-6 -->

Explanation:

**A39.** (a) iron(III) oxide; (b) ammonium nitrate; (c) potassium dichromate.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-7 -->

Explanation:

**A40.** Ionic bonding; high melting point: strong electrostatic attraction between oppositely charged ions throughout the lattice needs a lot of energy to overcome; conductivity: ions are fixed in the solid but free to move in solution, so only the solution conducts; brittleness: displacing layers aligns like charges, which repel (any two explanations).
<!-- id: chem12a1-ib-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Nanomaterials and reactivity of metals

**A41.** A. 1–100 nm
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-1 -->

Explanation: The nanoscale is about 1–100 nm (1 nm = 10⁻⁹ m). 0.01–0.1 nm is about the size of a single atom, and the µm range describes much larger particles, such as cells.

**A42.** B. 10 times larger.
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-2 -->

Explanation: Original: 6 × (1.0 cm)² = 6.0 cm². Each small cube: 6 × (0.10 cm)² = 0.060 cm². Total: 1000 × 0.060 cm² = 60 cm², which is 10 times larger. Dividing particles further increases the surface area to volume ratio, which is why nanoparticles are so reactive.

**A43.** C. Potassium
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-3 -->

Explanation: Potassium is a highly reactive group 1 metal: 2K(s) + 2H₂O(l) → 2KOH(aq) + H₂(g). Iron reacts only very slowly (rusting needs both oxygen and water), and copper and silver do not react with water.

**A44.** B. zinc chloride and hydrogen gas.
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-4 -->

Explanation: A reactive metal and a dilute acid produce a salt and hydrogen: Zn(s) + 2HCl(aq) → ZnCl₂(aq) + H₂(g). Chlorine is not produced, and the metal oxide or hydroxide does not form in acid.

**A45.** C. materials at the nanoscale can show different properties from the same material in bulk.
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-5 -->

Explanation: At the nanoscale, particle size affects how a material interacts with light, along with its other properties, so gold nanoparticles look red. The gold is still metallic gold, not ions or a new compound, and isotopes have no effect on colour.

**A46.** (a) Ca(s) + 2H₂O(l) → Ca(OH)₂(aq) + H₂(g); (b) 2Mg(s) + O₂(g) → 2MgO(s).
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-6 -->

Explanation:

**A47.** Order of reactivity: A > B > C; A could be magnesium (reacts rapidly with dilute acid); B could be iron (reacts slowly), and C could be copper (does not react with dilute HCl).
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-7 -->

Explanation:

**A48.** Nanoparticles have a much larger surface area to volume ratio, so a larger proportion of silver atoms are exposed at the surface; this allows more contact with and greater release of silver ions to bacteria, so a small mass is very effective; concern: nanoparticles may penetrate cells or tissue, accumulate in the body or the environment, or harm beneficial organisms, and their long-term health effects are not fully known.
<!-- id: chem12a1-nr-8 -->

Explanation:

### Unit 1 AOS 1: Covalent substances

#### Lewis structures and covalent bonding

**A49.** B. 1
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-1 -->

Explanation: N has 5 valence electrons. Three are used in N–H bonds, and the remaining two form one lone pair, which completes an octet around N.

**A50.** C. N₂
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-2 -->

Explanation: Each N atom has 5 valence electrons and needs 3 more, so the two atoms share three pairs (N≡N). O₂ contains a double bond, and H₂O and CH₄ contain only single bonds.

**A51.** B. 4
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-3 -->

Explanation: The structure is O=C=O. Carbon has no lone pairs, and each O has two lone pairs, giving 4 in total. 16 valence electrons − 8 bonding electrons = 8 electrons = 4 lone pairs.

**A52.** C. 6
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-4 -->

Explanation: Ethene has four C–H single bonds (4 pairs) and one C=C double bond (2 pairs), giving 6 bonding pairs. The 12 valence electrons are all used in bonding, so there are no lone pairs.

**A53.** D. the electrostatic attraction between a shared pair of electrons and the nuclei of the two bonded atoms.
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-5 -->

Explanation: In a covalent bond, two atoms (usually non-metals) share a pair of electrons, and both nuclei are attracted to it. The other options describe ionic bonding, metallic bonding and intermolecular forces.

**A54.** Carbon is the central atom; C forms a double bond to O and single bonds to each of the two H atoms; O has two lone pairs while C and H have none (C and O have octets, H has 2 electrons).
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-6 -->

Explanation:

**A55.** Valence electrons: 5 + 3 × 7 = 26; 3 bonding pairs (three P–Cl single bonds); lone pairs: 1 on P + 3 on each Cl = 10 lone pairs (26 − 6 = 20 electrons).
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-7 -->

Explanation:

**A56.** Melting only requires overcoming weak intermolecular (dispersion) forces between molecules, not the strong covalent bonds within them, so little energy is needed; the molecules are neutral and all electrons are localised in bonds or lone pairs, so there are no free charged particles to carry a current.
<!-- id: chem12a1-lc-8 -->

Explanation:

#### VSEPR and molecular shape

**A57.** D. trigonal pyramidal.
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-1 -->

Explanation: N has four electron pairs around it (3 bonding and 1 lone pair), arranged roughly tetrahedrally. Molecular shape describes only the positions of the atoms, so NH₃ is trigonal pyramidal. Trigonal planar would require three bonding pairs and no lone pairs.

**A58.** B. CO₂
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-2 -->

Explanation: In CO₂, carbon has two double bonds (two regions of electron density) and no lone pairs, so the two regions are 180° apart. H₂O and H₂S have lone pairs on the central atom and are V-shaped, and SO₂ is also bent.

**A59.** B. 104.5°
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-3 -->

Explanation: Water has four electron pairs around O (2 bonding, 2 lone). Lone pairs repel more strongly than bonding pairs, so the angle is squeezed from the tetrahedral 109.5° to about 104.5°.

**A60.** C. SiH₄
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-4 -->

Explanation: Si has four bonding pairs and no lone pairs, so the four H atoms sit at the corners of a tetrahedron. PH₃ is pyramidal, H₂S is V-shaped and HCN is linear.

**A61.** A. trigonal planar, 120°
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-5 -->

Explanation: A double bond counts as one region of electron density. Three regions with no lone pairs spread out in a flat triangle at about 120°. A pyramidal shape would need a lone pair on C.

**A62.** The O atom has four electron pairs (two bonding, two lone pairs) which repel each other and arrange themselves tetrahedrally to be as far apart as possible; the shape is described by the atoms only, so the two H atoms and O form a V (bent) shape with an angle of about 104.5°.
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-6 -->

Explanation:

**A63.** (a) HCN: linear, 180° (two regions of electron density around C, no lone pairs); (b) SiCl₄: tetrahedral, 109.5° (four bonding pairs, no lone pairs on Si).
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-7 -->

Explanation:

**A64.** Both central atoms have four electron pairs arranged tetrahedrally; N in ammonia has one lone pair, which repels more strongly than bonding pairs, pushing the N–H bonds closer together and reducing the angle, while methane has four bonding pairs and no lone pairs.
<!-- id: chem12a1-vs-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Electronegativity and polarity

**A65.** D. H–F
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-1 -->

Explanation: Fluorine is the most electronegative element, so the H–F bond has the largest electronegativity difference (about 1.8). C–H is almost non-polar, with a difference of about 0.4.

**A66.** C. CO₂
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-2 -->

Explanation: CO₂ is linear, so the two equal C=O dipoles point in opposite directions and cancel. NH₃ and H₂O are polar because their asymmetrical shapes leave a net dipole, and HCl has only one polar bond.

**A67.** B. Water, H₂O
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-3 -->

Explanation: Water molecules are polar, so they orient in the electric field and are attracted towards the rod. Hexane and cyclohexane are non-polar hydrocarbons, and CCl₄ is symmetrical with cancelling dipoles.

**A68.** A. Caesium and fluorine
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-4 -->

Explanation: Caesium has one of the lowest electronegativities and fluorine the highest, giving the largest electronegativity difference and therefore the greatest ionic character. Sodium iodide is also ionic, but with a smaller difference.

**A69.** B. the ability of an atom to attract the shared electrons in a covalent bond.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-5 -->

Explanation: Electronegativity measures how strongly an atom attracts bonding electrons towards itself. The first option defines first ionisation energy, which follows a related but different trend.

**A70.** Cl is more electronegative than C, so each C–Cl bond is polar (Cδ+–Clδ−); the molecule is tetrahedral and symmetrical, so the four equal bond dipoles cancel and there is no overall dipole.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-6 -->

Explanation:

**A71.** O is more electronegative than H, so the shared electrons in each O–H bond are pulled towards O, making each bond polar; O carries a partial negative charge (δ−) and each H a partial positive charge (δ+); the molecule is V-shaped (asymmetrical), so the bond dipoles do not cancel and there is a net dipole.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-7 -->

Explanation:

**A72.** H–Br < H–Cl < H–F; electronegativity decreases down group 17 because the valence shell is further from the nucleus (more shells, same core charge), so the halogen's attraction for the shared electrons, and therefore the electronegativity difference with H, decreases from F to Br.
<!-- id: chem12a1-ep-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Intermolecular forces

**A73.** B. hydrogen bonding.
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-1 -->

Explanation: Methanol has an H atom bonded directly to a highly electronegative O atom. This allows hydrogen bonding with lone pairs on neighbouring O atoms, which is stronger than dispersion and other dipole–dipole forces. Covalent bonds act within molecules, not between them.

**A74.** C. the larger number of electrons gives stronger dispersion forces.
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-2 -->

Explanation: Noble gases are non-polar monatomic particles, so only dispersion forces act between them. Larger atoms have more electrons, which form larger, more easily formed temporary dipoles, so the attraction is stronger.

**A75.** D. Chloromethane, CH₃Cl
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-3 -->

Explanation: Hydrogen bonding requires H bonded directly to N, O or F. In CH₃Cl the H atoms are bonded to C, so it shows only dipole–dipole and dispersion forces.

**A76.** B. the compact shape of 2,2-dimethylpropane reducing contact between molecules, so it has weaker dispersion forces.
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-4 -->

Explanation: Both molecules have the same number of electrons and are non-polar, so only dispersion forces act. The long chain of pentane gives a larger contact area between molecules, so its dispersion forces are stronger. Neither substance can hydrogen bond.

**A77.** A. water molecules form hydrogen bonds with each other.
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-5 -->

Explanation: Boiling separates molecules, so it depends on intermolecular forces, not bond strength within molecules. Water forms hydrogen bonds because O is small and highly electronegative, whereas S is not electronegative enough. H₂S actually has more electrons than H₂O and is V-shaped.

**A78.** In ice, each water molecule forms hydrogen bonds to four neighbours in a fixed, open hexagonal lattice; this arrangement holds molecules further apart than in the liquid; when ice melts some hydrogen bonds break and molecules pack more closely, so the same mass occupies a smaller volume in the liquid (ice has a lower density and floats).
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-6 -->

Explanation:

**A79.** (a) HF molecules form hydrogen bonds (H bonded to highly electronegative F), which are much stronger than the intermolecular forces in the other hydrogen halides; (b) HCl, HBr and HI cannot hydrogen bond; from HCl to HI the number of electrons increases, so dispersion forces become stronger, outweighing the decrease in dipole–dipole attraction as bond polarity falls.
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-7 -->

Explanation:

**A80.** (a) CH₄: dispersion forces only (non-polar); (b) HCl: dispersion and dipole–dipole forces; (c) NH₃: dispersion, dipole–dipole and hydrogen bonding.
<!-- id: chem12a1-im-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Covalent lattices and carbon allotropes

**A81.** B. covalently bonded to four other carbon atoms in a tetrahedral arrangement.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-1 -->

Explanation: Diamond is a three-dimensional covalent network in which every C forms four single covalent bonds arranged tetrahedrally. Bonding to three atoms in flat layers describes graphite, and C₆₀ is a fullerene.

**A82.** A. each carbon atom has one delocalised electron that can move along the layers.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-2 -->

Explanation: Each C in graphite forms three covalent bonds within its layer. The fourth valence electron is delocalised across the layer and can move when a voltage is applied. If all valence electrons were localised in bonds, as in diamond, graphite would not conduct.

**A83.** C. Silicon dioxide, SiO₂
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-3 -->

Explanation: In SiO₂ (quartz), each Si is covalently bonded to four O atoms and each O to two Si atoms in a continuous three-dimensional network. CO₂ and I₂ are covalent molecular substances, and NaCl is ionic.

**A84.** D. the weak dispersion forces between its layers allow the layers to slide over each other.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-4 -->

Explanation: Within each layer the covalent bonds are very strong, but the layers are held together only by weak dispersion forces, so they slide easily. Graphite actually has a very high sublimation point, above 3600 °C.

**A85.** A. a single layer of carbon atoms arranged in hexagons, as in one layer of graphite.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-5 -->

Explanation: Graphene is a one-atom-thick sheet of hexagonally bonded carbon. It is extremely strong, conducts electricity well and is nearly transparent. C₆₀ is buckminsterfullerene, and the tetrahedral network is diamond.

**A86.** In diamond each C is covalently bonded to four others in a rigid 3D tetrahedral network, so it is very hard, and all valence electrons are localised in bonds so it cannot conduct; in graphite each C bonds to three others in layers held together by weak dispersion forces, so layers slide and it is soft; the fourth electron of each C is delocalised within the layers, so graphite conducts.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-6 -->

Explanation:

**A87.** SiO₂ is a covalent network lattice, so melting requires breaking many strong covalent Si–O bonds throughout the structure; CO₂ consists of discrete non-polar molecules, so only weak dispersion forces between molecules must be overcome.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-7 -->

Explanation:

**A88.** Use: e.g. reinforcing lightweight sports equipment or composite materials, or as tiny electrical conductors in electronics; property and explanation: very high tensile strength because of the strong covalent bonds in the continuous hexagonal carbon network, or electrical conductivity because of the delocalised electrons in the graphene structure.
<!-- id: chem12a1-cl-8 -->

Explanation:

### Unit 1 AOS 2: Quantifying and classifying materials

#### Relative masses and mass spectrometry

**A89.** D. 77.5%
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-1 -->

Explanation: Let x be the fraction of ³⁵Cl: 35x + 37(1 − x) = 35.45, so 37 − 2x = 35.45 and x = 0.775, i.e. 77.5%. 22.5% is the abundance of ³⁷Cl, not ³⁵Cl.

**A90.** B. Positively charged ions, separated according to their mass-to-charge ratio
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-2 -->

Explanation: The sample is ionised to form positive ions, which are accelerated and deflected; the amount of deflection depends on m/z. Neutral atoms cannot be accelerated or deflected by electric or magnetic fields, so they are not detected.

**A91.** A. one-twelfth of the mass of an atom of carbon-12
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-3 -->

Explanation: The modern scale defines one-twelfth of the mass of a ¹²C atom as exactly 1 unit, so ¹²C has a relative isotopic mass of exactly 12. Hydrogen and oxygen scales were used historically but are not the current standard.

**A92.** B. 69.8
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-4 -->

Explanation: Ar = (69 × 60.1 + 71 × 39.9) ÷ 100 = (4146.9 + 2832.9) ÷ 100 = 69.8. 70.0 would be the simple average, which ignores the higher abundance of ⁶⁹Ga.

**A93.** C. a ⁷⁹Br–⁸¹Br molecule can form in two ways (⁷⁹Br first or ⁸¹Br first), so it is twice as likely as either ⁷⁹Br–⁷⁹Br or ⁸¹Br–⁸¹Br
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-5 -->

Explanation: With a 50 : 50 isotope ratio, the combinations 79–79, 79–81, 81–79 and 81–81 are equally likely, so the mixed molecule (m/z 160) appears twice as often. Ions with m/z 160 are deflected in between the other two, not the least.

**A94.** Ar = (24 × 78.99 + 25 × 10.00 + 26 × 11.01) ÷ 100; = (1895.76 + 250.0 + 286.26) ÷ 100 = 24.32 (no units, as it is a relative value).
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-6 -->

Explanation:

**A95.** Relative isotopic mass is the mass of one atom of a particular isotope relative to one-twelfth of the mass of a ¹²C atom; relative atomic mass is the weighted mean of the relative isotopic masses of all naturally occurring isotopes of the element, taking their abundances into account; because most elements are a mixture of isotopes, the weighted mean lies between the isotopic masses and is usually not a whole number.
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-7 -->

Explanation:

**A96.** Ar = (63 × 69.2 + 65 × 30.8) ÷ 100; = (4359.6 + 2002.0) ÷ 100 = 63.6; X is copper (Ar = 63.5 in the data book), the closest match.
<!-- id: chem12a2-rm-8 -->

Explanation:

#### The mole and molar mass

**A97.** B. 1.51 × 10²³
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-1 -->

Explanation: N = n × NA = 0.250 × 6.02 × 10²³ = 1.51 × 10²³. 2.41 × 10²⁴ results from dividing NA by n rather than multiplying.

**A98.** B. 164.1 g mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-2 -->

Explanation: M = 40.1 + 2 × (14.0 + 3 × 16.0) = 40.1 + 124.0 = 164.1 g mol⁻¹. 102.1 g mol⁻¹ results from forgetting the subscript 2 outside the brackets.

**A99.** D. 16.0 g of CH₄
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-3 -->

Explanation: Each of H₂O, CO₂ and CH₄ is 1.00 mol of molecules, but CH₄ has 5 atoms per molecule (5 mol of atoms) compared with 3 for H₂O and CO₂. 1.0 g of H₂ is 0.50 mol of molecules, or only 1.0 mol of atoms.

**A100.** A. 4.77 g
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-4 -->

Explanation: M(Na₂CO₃) = 2(23.0) + 12.0 + 3(16.0) = 106.0 g mol⁻¹; m = n × M = 0.0450 × 106.0 = 4.77 g. 4.25 × 10⁻⁴ g comes from dividing n by M.

**A101.** C. 7.2 × 10²³
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-5 -->

Explanation: Each formula unit contains 3 × 4 = 12 O atoms, so n(O) = 12 × 0.10 = 1.2 mol and N = 1.2 × 6.02 × 10²³ = 7.2 × 10²³. 2.4 × 10²³ counts only the 4 O in one sulfate ion.

**A102.** M(C₆H₁₂O₆) = 6(12.0) + 12(1.0) + 6(16.0) = 180.0 g mol⁻¹; n = 9.00 ÷ 180.0 = 0.0500 mol; N = 0.0500 × 6.02 × 10²³ = 3.01 × 10²² molecules.
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-6 -->

Explanation:

**A103.** n(H) = 1.20 × 10²⁴ ÷ 6.02 × 10²³ = 1.99 mol; each ethanol molecule has 6 H atoms, so n(C₂H₅OH) = 1.99 ÷ 6 = 0.332 mol; M(C₂H₅OH) = 46.0 g mol⁻¹, so m = 0.332 × 46.0 = 15.3 g.
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-7 -->

Explanation:

**A104.** One mole is the amount of substance containing 6.02 × 10²³ (Avogadro's number of) specified particles; both samples contain the same number of atoms, but each magnesium atom on average has about twice the mass of a carbon atom, so the molar mass (numerically equal to Ar in grams) is larger for magnesium.
<!-- id: chem12a2-mol-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Percentage composition and empirical formulas

**A105.** B. 35.0%
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-1 -->

Explanation: M(NH₄NO₃) = 2(14.0) + 4(1.0) + 3(16.0) = 80.0 g mol⁻¹; %N = 28.0 ÷ 80.0 × 100 = 35.0%. 17.5% counts only one of the two nitrogen atoms.

**A106.** D. CH₂O
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-2 -->

Explanation: In 100 g: C 40.0 ÷ 12.0 = 3.33 mol, H 6.7 ÷ 1.0 = 6.7 mol, O 53.3 ÷ 16.0 = 3.33 mol. Dividing by 3.33 gives 1 : 2 : 1, so CH₂O. CHO would ignore the 2 : 1 ratio of H to C.

**A107.** C. C₆H₁₂
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-3 -->

Explanation: M(CH₂) = 14.0 g mol⁻¹ and 84.0 ÷ 14.0 = 6, so the molecular formula is (CH₂)₆ = C₆H₁₂. C₆H₁₄ has the right number of C atoms but its empirical formula is C₃H₇, not CH₂.

**A108.** A. Ethyne, C₂H₂, and benzene, C₆H₆
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-4 -->

Explanation: Both C₂H₂ and C₆H₆ reduce to CH. Propane (C₃H₈) and butane (empirical formula C₂H₅) have different simplest ratios even though they are in the same homologous series.

**A109.** C. Some magnesium remained unreacted at the end of heating
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-5 -->

Explanation: Unreacted magnesium means less oxygen combined, so the mass gain (and the calculated amount of O) is too low, giving a ratio below 1 : 1. Absorbing water would increase the final mass and make the O value too high, not too low.

**A110.** In 100 g: n(C) = 85.6 ÷ 12.0 = 7.13 mol and n(H) = 14.4 ÷ 1.0 = 14.4 mol; ratio 7.13 : 14.4 = 1 : 2.02 ≈ 1 : 2, so the empirical formula is CH₂ (M = 14.0 g mol⁻¹); 56.0 ÷ 14.0 = 4, so the molecular formula is C₄H₈.
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-6 -->

Explanation:

**A111.** m(H₂O) lost = 2.50 − 1.60 = 0.90 g, so n(H₂O) = 0.90 ÷ 18.0 = 0.050 mol; M(CuSO₄) = 63.5 + 32.1 + 64.0 = 159.6 g mol⁻¹, so n(CuSO₄) = 1.60 ÷ 159.6 = 0.0100 mol; ratio H₂O : CuSO₄ = 0.050 : 0.0100 = 5, so x = 5 (CuSO₄·5H₂O).
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-7 -->

Explanation:

**A112.** M(Fe₂O₃) = 2(55.8) + 3(16.0) = 159.6 g mol⁻¹; %Fe = 111.6 ÷ 159.6 × 100 = 69.9%; m(Fe₂O₃) = 0.800 × 500 = 400 kg, so m(Fe) = 0.699 × 400 = 280 kg (2.80 × 10² kg).
<!-- id: chem12a2-emp-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Hydrocarbons and IUPAC naming

**A113.** B. 2-methylbutane
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-1 -->

Explanation: The longest chain has 4 carbons (butane) with a methyl branch; numbering from the end nearest the branch gives the lowest locant, 2. 3-methylbutane numbers from the wrong end.

**A114.** A. C₅H₁₀
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-2 -->

Explanation: Alkenes with one C=C bond have the general formula CₙH₂ₙ, which C₅H₁₀ fits. C₅H₁₂ and C₆H₁₄ fit CₙH₂ₙ₊₂ (alkanes), and C₅H₈ fits CₙH₂ₙ₋₂ (alkynes).

**A115.** A. 2
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-3 -->

Explanation: Only butane (unbranched) and 2-methylpropane are possible. Drawing a 'bent' four-carbon chain does not create a new isomer, which is a common source of over-counting.

**A116.** B. pent-2-ene
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-4 -->

Explanation: The chain has 5 carbons with a C=C between C2 and C3 when numbered to give the double bond the lowest locant, so it is pent-2-ene. Pent-3-ene numbers from the wrong end; '-yne' indicates a triple bond.

**A117.** D. the increasing strength of dispersion forces as the molecules become larger
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-5 -->

Explanation: Larger molecules have more electrons and greater surface contact, giving stronger dispersion forces that need more energy to overcome. Boiling does not break covalent bonds, and alkanes cannot hydrogen bond.

**A118.** (a) pentane; (b) but-1-yne; (c) 2-methylpropane (methylpropane is also accepted).
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-6 -->

Explanation:

**A119.** Both are non-polar, so the only intermolecular forces are dispersion forces, and both have the same number of electrons; pentane is a linear (unbranched) molecule, so its molecules have a larger surface area of contact and can approach more closely; this gives stronger dispersion forces in pentane, needing more energy to separate the molecules, hence a higher boiling point than the compact, spherical 2,2-dimethylpropane.
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-7 -->

Explanation:

**A120.** (a) C₃H₈(g) + 5O₂(g) → 3CO₂(g) + 4H₂O(l); (b) the chain should be numbered from the end that gives the branch the lowest locant; the correct name is 2-methylbutane.
<!-- id: chem12a2-hc-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Functional groups and homologous series

**A121.** C. Carboxyl group, –COOH
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-1 -->

Explanation: Carboxylic acids contain the carboxyl group, –COOH (a C=O and an –OH on the same carbon). Although an –OH is present, the compound is not classified as an alcohol.

**A122.** A. propan-1-ol
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-2 -->

Explanation: It is a three-carbon alcohol with the –OH on carbon 1, so propan-1-ol. Propan-2-ol would have the –OH on the middle carbon, CH₃CH(OH)CH₃.

**A123.** D. Successive members differ by a –CH₂– unit and have similar chemical properties
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-3 -->

Explanation: A homologous series shares a general formula and functional group, with successive members differing by CH₂; their physical properties (e.g. boiling point) change gradually. Isomers have the same molecular formula, which homologues do not.

**A124.** B. CH₃CH₂Cl
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-4 -->

Explanation: Chloroethane, CH₃CH₂Cl, has a halogen atom replacing a hydrogen in an alkane. CH₃OH is an alcohol and CH₃COOH is a carboxylic acid.

**A125.** C. Propan-1-ol, C₃H₇OH (60.0 g mol⁻¹)
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-5 -->

Explanation: Propan-1-ol molecules can form hydrogen bonds through their –OH group, which are much stronger than the dipole–dipole and dispersion forces in chloroethane or the dispersion forces in butane. Chloroethane has the highest molar mass but no hydrogen bonding.

**A126.** Ethanol has a polar –OH group with H bonded to highly electronegative O; it can form hydrogen bonds with water molecules, which release enough energy to compensate for breaking hydrogen bonds between water molecules, so ethanol mixes with water; ethane is non-polar with only dispersion forces, so it cannot form hydrogen bonds with water and would disrupt the water hydrogen-bond network without compensating attractions, so it does not dissolve.
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-6 -->

Explanation:

**A127.** Fifth member has n = 5: pentan-1-ol, C₅H₁₁OH (C₅H₁₂O); M = 5(12.0) + 12(1.0) + 16.0 = 88.0 g mol⁻¹.
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-7 -->

Explanation:

**A128.** (a) carboxyl group, propanoic acid; (b) bromo (halo) group, 2-bromopropane; (c) carbon–carbon double bond, propene (prop-1-ene).
<!-- id: chem12a2-fg-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Addition polymers

**A129.** A. A carbon–carbon double bond
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-1 -->

Explanation: In addition polymerisation one bond of the C=C breaks, allowing monomers to join into a chain with no other product. A halogen atom alone is not enough; chloroethene polymerises because of its C=C, not its Cl.

**A130.** D. –CH₂–CHCl–
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-2 -->

Explanation: The C=C opens and each monomer contributes a –CH₂–CHCl– unit with only single bonds in the chain. –CH=CCl– wrongly retains a double bond and has lost hydrogen.

**A131.** B. propene
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-3 -->

Explanation: Restoring the C=C between the two chain carbons gives CH₂=CHCH₃, propene. Propane has no double bond, so it cannot undergo addition polymerisation.

**A132.** C. has little chain branching, so chains pack closely and have stronger dispersion forces between them
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-4 -->

Explanation: Both are made from ethene; HDPE chains are largely unbranched, so they align closely in crystalline regions, increasing density and the strength of dispersion forces. Polyethene has no cross-links and no hydrogen bonding.

**A133.** B. 1.00 × 10⁴
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-5 -->

Explanation: M(C₂H₄) = 28.0 g mol⁻¹, and 2.80 × 10⁵ ÷ 28.0 = 1.00 × 10⁴. Because no atoms are lost in addition polymerisation, the chain mass is simply the number of units × 28.0.

**A134.** Monomer is tetrafluoroethene, CF₂=CF₂; repeating unit is –CF₂–CF₂–; the C=C double bond of each monomer breaks and monomers join end to end with no other product (no small molecule such as water is lost), so it is addition polymerisation.
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-6 -->

Explanation:

**A135.** Longer chains have more electrons and greater contact area between chains, so the dispersion forces between chains are stronger in total; more energy is needed for the chains to slide past each other, so the polymer softens at a higher temperature.
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-7 -->

Explanation:

**A136.** n CH₂=CH(C₆H₅) → –[CH₂–CH(C₆H₅)]ₙ–; the repeating unit shows the double bond opened and a benzene ring (phenyl group) on every second carbon; e.g. it is rigid/brittle at room temperature because the bulky phenyl groups restrict chain movement (or it is a thermoplastic that softens on heating because there are no cross-links).
<!-- id: chem12a2-ap-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Thermoplastics, thermosets and the circular economy

**A137.** D. Chains are held together only by intermolecular forces, so it softens on heating and can be remoulded
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-1 -->

Explanation: Heating a thermoplastic overcomes the relatively weak intermolecular forces between chains, letting them slide and be reshaped. The cross-linked description in option A is a thermoset.

**A138.** B. It has extensive covalent cross-links between polymer chains
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-2 -->

Explanation: Covalent cross-links lock thermoset chains in place, so heating cannot make them flow; strong heating eventually breaks covalent bonds and the material decomposes. Raw material source does not define thermosets.

**A139.** C. Designing products so their materials can be reused, repaired or recycled and kept in use for as long as possible
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-3 -->

Explanation: A circular economy aims to design out waste and keep materials in use. The other options follow the linear 'take–make–dispose' model or simply move waste elsewhere.

**A140.** A. Polylactic acid (PLA)
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-4 -->

Explanation: PLA is made from lactic acid obtained by fermenting plant sugars, and is compostable under industrial conditions. PVC, PTFE and polystyrene are normally made from fossil fuel-derived monomers.

**A141.** D. Its covalent cross-links prevent the chains from flowing, and heating strongly enough to break them decomposes the polymer
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-5 -->

Explanation: Melting requires chains to move past each other, which cross-links prevent; the material chars instead. Thermosets are carbon-based and do not dissolve in water.

**A142.** In a linear economy, fossil fuels are extracted to make PET, bottles are used once and then sent to landfill or become litter (take–make–dispose); in a circular economy, bottles are designed for recovery, collected (e.g. through container deposit schemes) and mechanically or chemically recycled into new bottles; this reduces demand for new raw materials, energy use and waste, keeping the material in use for longer.
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-6 -->

Explanation:

**A143.** The resin chains are joined by covalent cross-links forming a rigid three-dimensional network; heating gives the chains energy, but the strong covalent cross-links prevent them from sliding past one another, so the material does not soften or melt; only at very high temperatures do covalent bonds break, causing decomposition (charring) rather than melting.
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-7 -->

Explanation:

**A144.** Bio-based means the carbon comes from a renewable biological feedstock (sugarcane); however, bio-PE is chemically identical to fossil-derived polyethene, so microorganisms cannot readily break it down and it is not biodegradable; an advantage is that it reduces use of finite fossil fuels, and the CO₂ absorbed by the sugarcane during growth can offset some of the emissions (and it can be recycled with ordinary PE).
<!-- id: chem12a2-tt-8 -->

Explanation:

### Unit 2 AOS 1: Chemicals and water

#### Properties of water and hydrogen bonding

**A145.** C. extensive hydrogen bonding between molecules absorbs energy that would otherwise raise the kinetic energy of the molecules
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-1 -->

Explanation: Much of the energy supplied is used to disrupt hydrogen bonds, so a larger energy input is needed to raise the temperature. Heating water does not break covalent O–H bonds, and water is polar.

**A146.** A. 62.7 kJ
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-2 -->

Explanation: q = mcΔT = 250 × 4.18 × 60.0 = 6.27 × 10⁴ J = 62.7 kJ. 20.9 kJ uses the initial temperature (20.0 °C) instead of ΔT; 6.27 × 10⁴ kJ forgets to convert J to kJ.

**A147.** D. hydrogen bonding holds the molecules in an open hexagonal lattice, so ice is less dense than liquid water
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-3 -->

Explanation: In ice each molecule hydrogen-bonds to four neighbours in a regular, open arrangement with more space between molecules than in the liquid. The molar mass of H₂O is the same in both states.

**A148.** B. H₂O, because its molecules form hydrogen bonds
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-4 -->

Explanation: Water boils at 100 °C, far higher than the others, because O is highly electronegative and small, allowing strong hydrogen bonds. H₂Te has the strongest dispersion forces of the group but these are weaker than water's hydrogen bonding.

**A149.** C. 20.4 kJ
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-5 -->

Explanation: n(H₂O) = 9.00 ÷ 18.0 = 0.500 mol; energy = 0.500 × 40.7 = 20.4 kJ. 366 kJ results from multiplying by the mass in grams rather than the amount in mol.

**A150.** Water has a high specific heat capacity; this is because energy is used to disrupt the extensive hydrogen bonding between water molecules, so a large amount of energy is needed to change its temperature; large bodies of water such as Port Phillip Bay therefore absorb heat during the day and release it at night with only small temperature changes, moderating the air temperature of nearby land.
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-6 -->

Explanation:

**A151.** A hydrogen bond is an attraction between a hydrogen atom bonded to a highly electronegative atom (O) in one molecule and a lone pair on an O atom of a neighbouring molecule; the large electronegativity difference makes H strongly δ+ and O strongly δ−; each water molecule has two H atoms (each can bond to another molecule) and two lone pairs on O (each can accept a hydrogen bond), giving up to four hydrogen bonds.
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-7 -->

Explanation:

**A152.** ΔT = q ÷ (mc) = 15 000 ÷ (200 × 4.18) = 17.9 °C; final temperature = 18.0 + 17.9 = 35.9 °C.
<!-- id: chem12a2-wat-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Solubility and precipitation reactions

**A153.** B. AgCl
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-1 -->

Explanation: Silver chloride is a classic insoluble chloride. All sodium, potassium, ammonium and nitrate compounds are soluble.

**A154.** C. Ba²⁺(aq) + SO₄²⁻(aq) → BaSO₄(s)
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-2 -->

Explanation: The ionic equation shows only the ions that form the precipitate, omitting spectator ions. Option B is the full (molecular) equation, not the ionic equation; NaCl and BaCl₂ are soluble.

**A155.** A. Na⁺ and NO₃⁻
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-3 -->

Explanation: Ag⁺ and Cl⁻ combine to form AgCl(s); Na⁺ and NO₃⁻ remain in solution unchanged. Option D wrongly includes Cl⁻, which is consumed in the precipitate.

**A156.** D. 78 g
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-4 -->

Explanation: At 60 °C 110 g is dissolved; at 20 °C only 32 g can stay dissolved, so 110 − 32 = 78 g crystallises. 32 g is the amount that stays in solution.

**A157.** B. Pb(NO₃)₂(aq) and KI(aq)
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-5 -->

Explanation: Pb²⁺ and I⁻ form insoluble yellow PbI₂(s). In the other pairs every possible combination of ions (e.g. NaNO₃, KCl, KNO₃) is soluble.

**A158.** Water molecules are polar; the δ− oxygen ends are attracted to Na⁺ ions and the δ+ hydrogen ends to Cl⁻ ions at the surface of the lattice (ion–dipole attractions); these attractions are strong enough to overcome the ionic bonding, so ions are pulled from the lattice and become surrounded by water molecules (hydrated), dispersing through the solution as Na⁺(aq) and Cl⁻(aq).
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-6 -->

Explanation:

**A159.** Cu²⁺(aq) + 2OH⁻(aq) → Cu(OH)₂(s); a pale blue precipitate forms (and the blue colour of the solution fades); the spectator ions are Na⁺ and SO₄²⁻.
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-7 -->

Explanation:

**A160.** The ion is Ba²⁺; barium sulfate is insoluble, so Ba²⁺(aq) + SO₄²⁻(aq) → BaSO₄(s) explains the white precipitate; if Ag⁺ were present, adding chloride would have formed a white precipitate of AgCl, but none formed (BaCl₂ is soluble).
<!-- id: chem12a2-sol-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Brønsted-Lowry acids and bases

**A161.** A. proton donor
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-1 -->

Explanation: A Brønsted–Lowry acid donates H⁺ (a proton) and a base accepts it. Producing OH⁻ in water is the older Arrhenius description of a base.

**A162.** C. HPO₄²⁻
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-2 -->

Explanation: A conjugate base has one fewer H⁺ than its acid: H₂PO₄⁻ − H⁺ = HPO₄²⁻. H₃PO₄ is its conjugate acid.

**A163.** B. HCO₃⁻
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-3 -->

Explanation: HCO₃⁻ can donate a proton (forming CO₃²⁻) or accept one (forming H₂CO₃). CO₃²⁻ has no H to donate, and Cl⁻ is an extremely weak base that does not act as an acid.

**A164.** D. an acid, because it donates a proton to NH₃
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-4 -->

Explanation: H₂O loses H⁺ to become OH⁻, so it is the acid; NH₃ is the base. OH⁻ is the conjugate base of water, not the other way round.

**A165.** C. H₂SO₄
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-5 -->

Explanation: Sulfuric acid can donate two protons per molecule. Ethanoic acid has four H atoms but only the one in –COOH is ionisable, so it is monoprotic; H₃PO₄ is triprotic.

**A166.** (a) HCO₃⁻(aq) + OH⁻(aq) → CO₃²⁻(aq) + H₂O(l); (b) HCO₃⁻(aq) + H₃O⁺(aq) → H₂CO₃(aq) + H₂O(l) (or → CO₂(g) + 2H₂O(l)); an amphiprotic species can act as either a proton donor (acid) or a proton acceptor (base) depending on what it reacts with.
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-6 -->

Explanation:

**A167.** CH₃COOH / CH₃COO⁻ (acid / conjugate base); H₃O⁺ / H₂O (conjugate acid / base).
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-7 -->

Explanation:

**A168.** (a) MgCO₃(s) + 2HCl(aq) → MgCl₂(aq) + H₂O(l) + CO₂(g); (b) Zn(s) + 2HCl(aq) → ZnCl₂(aq) + H₂(g); in both, bubbles of gas (effervescence) are seen and the solid dissolves.
<!-- id: chem12a2-bl-8 -->

Explanation:

#### pH and strong vs weak acids

**A169.** B. 2.0
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-1 -->

Explanation: HCl is a strong acid and ionises completely, so [H₃O⁺] = 0.010 mol L⁻¹ and pH = −log₁₀(0.010) = 2.0. 12.0 would be the pH of 0.010 mol L⁻¹ NaOH.

**A170.** D. 1.0 × 10⁻⁴ mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-2 -->

Explanation: [H₃O⁺] = 10⁻ᵖᴴ = 10⁻⁴·⁰ = 1.0 × 10⁻⁴ mol L⁻¹. 1.0 × 10⁻¹⁰ mol L⁻¹ is the hydroxide ion concentration at 25 °C.

**A171.** A. The HCl solution has the lower pH because HCl ionises completely in water, whereas CH₃COOH only partially ionises
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-3 -->

Explanation: Strength refers to the extent of ionisation: HCl is strong, so [H₃O⁺] ≈ 0.10 mol L⁻¹, while ethanoic acid is weak and produces a much smaller [H₃O⁺]. Equal concentrations do not mean equal pH.

**A172.** C. 3.0
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-4 -->

Explanation: The solution is diluted 100-fold, so [H₃O⁺] falls from 0.10 to 1.0 × 10⁻³ mol L⁻¹ and pH = 3.0. Each tenfold dilution of a strong acid raises the pH by 1, not by 10.

**A173.** D. 12.0
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-5 -->

Explanation: Each Ba(OH)₂ releases 2 OH⁻, so [OH⁻] = 0.010 mol L⁻¹; [H₃O⁺] = 1.0 × 10⁻¹⁴ ÷ 0.010 = 1.0 × 10⁻¹² mol L⁻¹, pH = 12.0. 11.7 ignores the factor of 2.

**A174.** Strength describes the extent to which an acid ionises (donates protons) in water: a strong acid such as HCl ionises completely, while a weak acid such as CH₃COOH ionises only partially; concentration describes the amount of acid dissolved per litre of solution (mol L⁻¹); so an acid can be strong but dilute (e.g. 0.001 mol L⁻¹ HCl) or weak but concentrated (e.g. 10 mol L⁻¹ CH₃COOH).
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-6 -->

Explanation:

**A175.** n(HCl) = 0.100 × 0.0200 = 2.00 × 10⁻³ mol and n(NaOH) = 0.100 × 0.0150 = 1.50 × 10⁻³ mol, so H₃O⁺ is in excess by 5.0 × 10⁻⁴ mol; total volume = 35.0 mL, so [H₃O⁺] = 5.0 × 10⁻⁴ ÷ 0.0350 = 1.43 × 10⁻² mol L⁻¹; pH = −log₁₀(1.43 × 10⁻²) = 1.85.
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-7 -->

Explanation:

**A176.** [OH⁻] = 0.025 mol L⁻¹, so [H₃O⁺] = 1.0 × 10⁻¹⁴ ÷ 0.025 = 4.0 × 10⁻¹³ mol L⁻¹; pH = −log₁₀(4.0 × 10⁻¹³) = 12.40 (12.4).
<!-- id: chem12a2-ph-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Redox reactions and half-equations

**A177.** B. Cu²⁺(aq)
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-1 -->

Explanation: Cu²⁺ gains electrons (is reduced) to form Cu, so it is the oxidising agent. Zn(s) loses electrons and is the reducing agent, not the oxidising agent.

**A178.** D. +7
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-2 -->

Explanation: Each O is −2, giving −8 in total. The ion's charge is −1, so Mn + (−8) = −1 and Mn = +7. +2 is the oxidation number of Mn in Mn²⁺, the usual reduction product.

**A179.** C. Cr₂O₇²⁻(aq) + 14H⁺(aq) + 6e⁻ → 2Cr³⁺(aq) + 7H₂O(l)
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-3 -->

Explanation: Balance Cr (2), O with 7 H₂O, H with 14 H⁺, then charge: left side 14 − 2 = +12, right side +6, so 6 e⁻ are added to the left. Electrons on the product side would make it an oxidation, and 8 e⁻ leaves the charge unbalanced.

**A180.** B. AgNO₃(aq) + NaCl(aq) → AgCl(s) + NaNO₃(aq)
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-4 -->

Explanation: The precipitation of AgCl involves no change in oxidation numbers (Ag stays +1, Cl stays −1), so it is not redox. In the other reactions, elements change oxidation number, e.g. Fe goes from +3 to 0.

**A181.** D. is oxidised, from +4 to +6
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-5 -->

Explanation: In SO₂, S = +4; in SO₄²⁻, S + 4(−2) = −2 so S = +6. An increase in oxidation number is oxidation. An increase in oxidation number can never be reduction.

**A182.** Oxidation: Al(s) → Al³⁺(aq) + 3e⁻; Reduction: Cu²⁺(aq) + 2e⁻ → Cu(s); Overall (electrons balanced at 6): 2Al(s) + 3Cu²⁺(aq) → 2Al³⁺(aq) + 3Cu(s).
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-6 -->

Explanation:

**A183.** Reduction: MnO₄⁻(aq) + 8H⁺(aq) + 5e⁻ → Mn²⁺(aq) + 4H₂O(l); Oxidation: Fe²⁺(aq) → Fe³⁺(aq) + e⁻; Multiply the oxidation by 5 so electrons cancel; Overall: MnO₄⁻(aq) + 8H⁺(aq) + 5Fe²⁺(aq) → Mn²⁺(aq) + 5Fe³⁺(aq) + 4H₂O(l).
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-7 -->

Explanation:

**A184.** Cl in Cl₂ has oxidation number 0; in Cl⁻ it is −1, so some chlorine is reduced; in OCl⁻ it is +1, so some chlorine is oxidised; the same species is both oxidised and reduced (disproportionation), and H, O and Na oxidation numbers do not change.
<!-- id: chem12a3-rr-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Reactivity series and metal displacement

**A185.** C. Zinc
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-1 -->

Explanation: Zinc is a stronger reducing agent than iron (Zn²⁺/Zn lies below Fe²⁺/Fe in the electrochemical series), so it reduces Fe²⁺ to Fe. Lead is a weaker reductant than iron, so it does not react, and neither do Cu or Ag.

**A186.** B. Grey-silver crystals form on the copper and the solution turns blue
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-2 -->

Explanation: Cu is a stronger reductant than Ag, so Cu(s) + 2Ag⁺(aq) → Cu²⁺(aq) + 2Ag(s). Silver deposits form and blue Cu²⁺ ions appear. Copper is more reactive than silver, so 'no change' is wrong.

**A187.** A. Mg(s)
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-3 -->

Explanation: Mg²⁺/Mg has the most negative E° (−2.37 V) of these, so Mg(s) loses electrons most readily. Ag(s) is the weakest reductant of the four.

**A188.** D. X > Y > Z
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-4 -->

Explanation: X displaces Y, so X is more reactive than Y. Z cannot displace either, so Z is the least reactive. Putting Z first reverses what the 'no reaction' results mean.

**A189.** B. potassium reacts vigorously with the water itself, producing hydrogen gas and hydroxide ions
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-5 -->

Explanation: Potassium is such a strong reductant that it reduces water (2K + 2H₂O → 2K⁺ + 2OH⁻ + H₂) rather than the metal ions, often violently. K⁺ is actually a very weak oxidising agent.

**A190.** A reaction occurs; Pb²⁺(aq) (E° = −0.13 V) is a stronger oxidising agent than Zn²⁺(aq) (E° = −0.76 V), so Zn(s) is a stronger reductant than Pb(s) and can reduce Pb²⁺; Zn(s) + Pb²⁺(aq) → Zn²⁺(aq) + Pb(s) (grey crystals of lead form as zinc dissolves).
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-6 -->

Explanation:

**A191.** Iron is a stronger reducing agent than copper (Fe²⁺/Fe is below Cu²⁺/Cu in the electrochemical series), so iron is oxidised and the container corrodes while copper metal deposits; Fe(s) + Cu²⁺(aq) → Fe²⁺(aq) + Cu(s).
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-7 -->

Explanation:

**A192.** (a) A > C > B > D, since each metal displaces only those below it; (b) D²⁺ is the strongest oxidising agent; because D is the weakest reductant, its ion most readily gains electrons (every other metal can reduce D²⁺).
<!-- id: chem12a3-rs-8 -->

Explanation:

### Unit 2 AOS 2: Measuring and analysing chemicals

#### Concentration units and dilution

**A193.** C. 0.400 mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-1 -->

Explanation: n = 5.85 ÷ 58.5 = 0.100 mol; c = 0.100 ÷ 0.2500 L = 0.400 mol L⁻¹. 0.100 mol L⁻¹ forgets to divide by the volume in litres.

**A194.** A. 0.100 mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-2 -->

Explanation: c₂ = c₁V₁ ÷ V₂ = 2.00 × 25.0 ÷ 500.0 = 0.100 mol L⁻¹. The amount of HCl is unchanged by dilution; only the volume increases twenty-fold.

**A195.** B. 15 mg L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-3 -->

Explanation: 1 ppm = 1 mg per kg, and 1 L of dilute aqueous solution has a mass of about 1 kg, so 15 ppm = 15 mg L⁻¹. 15 µg L⁻¹ would be 15 ppb.

**A196.** D. 0.600 mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-4 -->

Explanation: Each Al₂(SO₄)₃ releases 3 SO₄²⁻ ions, so [SO₄²⁻] = 3 × 0.200 = 0.600 mol L⁻¹. 0.400 mol L⁻¹ is the Al³⁺ concentration.

**A197.** B. 4.00 % (m/v)
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-5 -->

Explanation: % (m/v) = grams of solute per 100 mL: 12.0 g ÷ 300 mL × 100 = 4.00 %. 0.0400 omits the ×100 conversion.

**A198.** V₁ = c₂V₂ ÷ c₁ = 0.250 × 2.00 ÷ 10.0 = 0.0500 L = 50.0 mL of stock; measure 50.0 mL accurately (pipette/burette), transfer to a 2.00 L volumetric flask and make up to the mark with deionised water, then mix.
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-6 -->

Explanation:

**A199.** (a) 0.48 mg ÷ 0.200 L = 2.4 mg L⁻¹ = 2.4 ppm; (b) M(NO₃⁻) = 14.0 + 3 × 16.0 = 62.0 g mol⁻¹; c = 2.4 × 10⁻³ g L⁻¹ ÷ 62.0 g mol⁻¹ = 3.9 × 10⁻⁵ mol L⁻¹ (2 significant figures).
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-7 -->

Explanation:

**A200.** First dilution: 0.500 × 10.0 ÷ 100.0 = 0.0500 mol L⁻¹; second dilution: 0.0500 × 5.00 ÷ 50.0 = 0.00500 mol L⁻¹ (5.00 × 10⁻³ mol L⁻¹); n(Cu²⁺) = 0.00500 × 0.0500 L = 2.50 × 10⁻⁴ mol (equal to the moles in the 5.00 mL aliquot).
<!-- id: chem12a3-cd-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Stoichiometry and limiting reactants

**A201.** B. 22 g
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-1 -->

Explanation: n(CH₄) = 8.0 ÷ 16.0 = 0.50 mol; the mole ratio is 1:1, so n(CO₂) = 0.50 mol; m = 0.50 × 44.0 = 22 g. 44 g assumes 1 mol of methane.

**A202.** B. O₂ is the limiting reactant
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-2 -->

Explanation: n(H₂) = 2.0 mol and n(O₂) = 0.500 mol. 2.0 mol H₂ needs 1.0 mol O₂, but only 0.500 mol is present, so O₂ is limiting. Comparing masses (4.0 g vs 16.0 g) wrongly suggests H₂ is limiting.

**A203.** D. 0.0250 mol
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-3 -->

Explanation: n(Zn) = 0.0500 mol and n(HCl) = 0.0500 mol. Zn needs 0.100 mol HCl, so HCl is limiting; n(H₂) = ½ × 0.0500 = 0.0250 mol. 0.0500 mol assumes Zn is limiting.

**A204.** C. 75.0 %
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-4 -->

Explanation: Percentage yield = actual ÷ theoretical × 100 = 9.00 ÷ 12.0 × 100 = 75.0 %. 133 % comes from inverting the ratio, and a yield above 100 % signals an error.

**A205.** A. 0.90 mol
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-5 -->

Explanation: n(CO) = 3/2 × n(Fe) = 1.5 × 0.60 = 0.90 mol. 0.40 mol uses the ratio upside down (2/3).

**A206.** n(Ag⁺) = 0.200 × 0.0500 = 0.0100 mol; n(Cl⁻) = 0.150 × 0.0300 = 0.00450 mol; the ratio is 1:1, so Cl⁻ (NaCl) is limiting; m(AgCl) = 0.00450 × 143.4 = 0.645 g; excess Ag⁺ = 0.0100 − 0.00450 = 0.00550 mol.
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-6 -->

Explanation:

**A207.** n(CaCO₃) = 2.50 ÷ 100.1 = 0.0250 mol; n(HCl) = 1.00 × 0.0400 = 0.0400 mol; 0.0250 mol CaCO₃ would need 0.0500 mol HCl, so HCl is limiting; n(CO₂) = ½ × 0.0400 = 0.0200 mol, m = 0.0200 × 44.0 = 0.880 g.
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-7 -->

Explanation:

**A208.** Any two of: the reaction may not go to completion (e.g. it is reversible or too slow); side reactions form other products; product is lost during transfer, filtration or washing; reactants are impure, so fewer moles are present than assumed.
<!-- id: chem12a3-sl-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Gas laws and molar volume

**A209.** B. 6.20 L
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-1 -->

Explanation: V = n × Vm = 0.250 × 24.8 = 6.20 L. 5.60 L uses 22.4 L mol⁻¹, the molar volume at STP (0 °C), not at SLC.

**A210.** D. 400 kPa
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-2 -->

Explanation: Boyle's law: P₁V₁ = P₂V₂, so P₂ = 100 × 2.0 ÷ 0.50 = 400 kPa. Reducing the volume to a quarter quadruples the pressure; 25 kPa wrongly assumes pressure falls.

**A211.** A. 0.120 mol
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-3 -->

Explanation: n = PV ÷ RT = (150 kPa × 2.00 L) ÷ (8.31 × 300) = 300 ÷ 2493 = 0.120 mol. kPa × L gives J, so no extra unit conversion is needed. 0.0120 mol comes from a units slip.

**A212.** C. 667 mL
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-4 -->

Explanation: Charles' law needs kelvin: V₂ = 500 × 400 K ÷ 300 K = 667 mL. 2350 mL comes from using Celsius temperatures (500 × 127 ÷ 27).

**A213.** B. number of molecules
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-5 -->

Explanation: Avogadro's hypothesis: equal volumes of gases at the same T and P contain equal numbers of particles (molecules). The atom count differs because H₂ is diatomic and CO₂ is triatomic, and the masses differ too.

**A214.** n(CO₂) = V ÷ Vm = 3.10 ÷ 24.8 = 0.125 mol; m = 0.125 × 44.0 = 5.50 g.
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-6 -->

Explanation:

**A215.** T = 298 K; n(N₂) = PV ÷ RT = (120 × 60.0) ÷ (8.31 × 298) = 2.91 mol; n(NaN₃) = 2/3 × 2.91 = 1.94 mol; M(NaN₃) = 23.0 + 3 × 14.0 = 65.0 g mol⁻¹, so m = 1.94 × 65.0 = 126 g.
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-7 -->

Explanation:

**A216.** Heating increases the average kinetic energy of the gas particles, so they move faster; they collide with the container walls more often and with greater force; because the volume is fixed, the force per unit area of wall (pressure) increases.
<!-- id: chem12a3-gl-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Volumetric analysis and titration

**A217.** B. Pipette
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-1 -->

Explanation: A pipette delivers a single fixed, accurate volume (the aliquot). The burette delivers the titrant and measures the titre. A measuring cylinder is not accurate enough.

**A218.** C. too high, because the diluted titrant gives a larger titre
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-2 -->

Explanation: Water left in the burette dilutes the titrant, so a larger volume is needed to reach the end point. The calculation uses the original (higher) concentration, which overestimates the moles of titrant and hence the analyte concentration.

**A219.** A. 0.0920 mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-3 -->

Explanation: n(HCl) = 0.100 × 0.01840 = 1.84 × 10⁻³ mol = n(NaOH) (1:1 ratio); c = 1.84 × 10⁻³ ÷ 0.02000 = 0.0920 mol L⁻¹. 0.109 mol L⁻¹ comes from inverting the volumes.

**A220.** D. 0.0675 mol L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-4 -->

Explanation: n(NaOH) = 0.150 × 0.02250 = 3.375 × 10⁻³ mol; n(H₂SO₄) = ½ × that = 1.6875 × 10⁻³ mol; c = 1.6875 × 10⁻³ ÷ 0.02500 = 0.0675 mol L⁻¹. 0.135 mol L⁻¹ ignores the 1:2 ratio.

**A221.** B. 21.35 mL, 21.40 mL, 21.30 mL
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-5 -->

Explanation: The range of 21.30 to 21.40 mL is 0.10 mL, so these three are concordant. The first set contains 22.10 mL, which is often a rough first titre and should be left out of the mean.

**A222.** n(NaOH) = 0.1050 × 0.01685 = 1.769 × 10⁻³ mol = n(CH₃COOH) in the aliquot (1:1); c(diluted) = 1.769 × 10⁻³ ÷ 0.02000 = 0.08846 mol L⁻¹; c(original) = 0.08846 × 10 (dilution factor 250.0/25.00) = 0.885 mol L⁻¹; 0.885 × 60.0 = 53.1 g L⁻¹ = 5.31 % (m/v).
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-6 -->

Explanation:

**A223.** Phenolphthalein; at equivalence the solution contains ethanoate ions, a weak base, so the equivalence point pH is above 7 (about 8–9); the indicator must change colour within the steep pH change at equivalence, which phenolphthalein does, whereas methyl orange would change colour well before equivalence, giving a titre that is too small.
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-7 -->

Explanation:

**A224.** Any three: high purity; known, stable chemical formula; does not absorb water or react with air (not hygroscopic or deliquescent); high molar mass to reduce weighing error; readily soluble; NaOH is unsuitable because it absorbs water from the air and reacts with atmospheric CO₂ (forming Na₂CO₃), so its mass does not reliably match the amount of NaOH.
<!-- id: chem12a3-vt-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Gravimetric analysis

**A225.** C. ensure all water has been removed from the precipitate
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-1 -->

Explanation: Heating to constant mass confirms that no more water is being lost, so the recorded mass is of dry precipitate only. Removing soluble impurities is the job of washing, not drying.

**A226.** A. ensure that all chloride ions are precipitated as AgCl
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-2 -->

Explanation: An excess of Ag⁺ makes chloride the limiting reactant, so all of it is precipitated and the mass of AgCl reflects the total chloride. Na⁺ does not react with Ag⁺.

**A227.** B. 3.55 %
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-3 -->

Explanation: m(Cl) = 0.287 × 35.5 ÷ 143.4 = 0.0711 g; % = 0.0711 ÷ 2.00 × 100 = 3.55 %. 14.4 % takes the mass of AgCl as if it were all chloride.

**A228.** D. It will be too high, because the dried precipitate mass includes NaCl
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-4 -->

Explanation: Impurities that dry onto the precipitate add mass, which is wrongly counted as BaSO₄, so the sulfate result is too high. NaCl being soluble does not help unless it is washed off.

**A229.** C. 0.192 g
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-5 -->

Explanation: n(BaSO₄) = 0.466 ÷ 233.4 = 2.00 × 10⁻³ mol = n(SO₄²⁻); m = 2.00 × 10⁻³ × 96.1 = 0.192 g. 0.466 g is the mass of the whole precipitate, including barium.

**A230.** m(H₂O) = 2.50 − 1.60 = 0.90 g, n(H₂O) = 0.90 ÷ 18.0 = 0.050 mol; n(CuSO₄) = 1.60 ÷ 159.6 = 0.0100 mol; ratio H₂O : CuSO₄ = 0.050 ÷ 0.0100 ≈ 5, so x = 5 (CuSO₄·5H₂O).
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-6 -->

Explanation:

**A231.** n(BaSO₄) = 0.0583 ÷ 233.4 = 2.50 × 10⁻⁴ mol; n(SO₄²⁻) = 2.50 × 10⁻⁴ mol (1:1); m(SO₄²⁻) = 2.50 × 10⁻⁴ × 96.1 = 0.0240 g = 24.0 mg; concentration = 24.0 mg ÷ 0.2000 L = 1.20 × 10² mg L⁻¹ = 120 ppm.
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-7 -->

Explanation:

**A232.** Any two with effect: the precipitate is not dried to constant mass, so the mass is too high and the result is overestimated; fine precipitate passes through the filter paper, or some precipitate stays dissolved, so the mass is too low and the result is underestimated; soluble impurities are not washed off or are co-precipitated, so the result is too high; not enough precipitating agent is added, so precipitation is incomplete and the result is too low.
<!-- id: chem12a3-ga-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Colorimetry and UV-visible spectroscopy

**A233.** B. Orange light, because the solution absorbs it most strongly
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-1 -->

Explanation: A solution looks blue because it transmits blue light and absorbs the complementary colour, orange. Measuring at the wavelength absorbed most strongly gives the greatest sensitivity. Blue light would mostly pass straight through.

**A234.** A. 2.6 mg L⁻¹
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-2 -->

Explanation: Absorbance is proportional to concentration: c = 0.26 ÷ 0.40 × 4.0 = 2.6 mg L⁻¹. 6.2 mg L⁻¹ comes from inverting the ratio.

**A235.** D. set absorbance to zero, so that any absorbance by the solvent and cuvette is not counted
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-3 -->

Explanation: The blank sets the baseline (A = 0), so measured absorbances come only from the analyte. It does not set the wavelength or measure a concentration.

**A236.** C. dilute the sample by a known factor, remeasure, and multiply by the dilution factor
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-4 -->

Explanation: Results are only reliable within the range of the standards, because the absorbance–concentration relationship may stop being linear. Diluting by a known factor brings the reading within range. Extrapolation assumes linearity that may not hold.

**A237.** B. electrons to move to higher energy levels
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-5 -->

Explanation: UV and visible photons have the right energy to promote electrons to higher energy levels. Bond vibrations are caused by infrared radiation, and fragmentation into ions describes mass spectrometry.

**A238.** The calibration is linear with slope 0.11 ÷ 2.0 = 0.055 per mg L⁻¹; diluted sample c = 0.15 ÷ 0.055 = 2.7 mg L⁻¹; dilution factor 50.0 ÷ 5.0 = 10, so original = 27 mg L⁻¹.
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-6 -->

Explanation:

**A239.** At λmax the absorbance changes most for a given change in concentration, giving the greatest sensitivity and the lowest detection limit; the absorption peak is flattest there, so small shifts in wavelength have little effect on readings, which improves precision (it may also reduce interference from other species).
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-7 -->

Explanation:

**A240.** Absorbance depends on the instrument, wavelength, cuvette path length and the analyte, so the relationship between absorbance and concentration must be found under the same conditions; the curve converts sample absorbance into concentration; the relationship is only known to be linear over the range measured, and at high concentrations it may stop being linear, so extrapolating beyond the standards is unreliable.
<!-- id: chem12a3-cu-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Greenhouse gases and ocean acidification

**A241.** C. Nitrogen, N₂
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-1 -->

Explanation: N₂ is a symmetrical diatomic molecule that does not absorb infrared radiation, so it is not a greenhouse gas. Water vapour is the most abundant greenhouse gas.

**A242.** B. absorb infrared radiation emitted from Earth's surface and re-emit some of it back towards the surface
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-2 -->

Explanation: Earth's surface emits infrared radiation; greenhouse gases absorb it and re-radiate energy in all directions, including back down. UV absorption is mainly due to ozone, and ozone depletion is a separate issue.

**A243.** D. It decreases, because the extra H₃O⁺ reacts with CO₃²⁻ to form HCO₃⁻
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-3 -->

Explanation: Dissolved CO₂ forms H₂CO₃, which donates protons; the extra H₃O⁺ reacts with carbonate (H₃O⁺ + CO₃²⁻ → HCO₃⁻ + H₂O), lowering [CO₃²⁻]. Buffering slows the pH change but does not stop carbonate being used up.

**A244.** A. Organisms that build calcium carbonate shells or skeletons, such as corals and molluscs
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-4 -->

Explanation: Lower carbonate ion concentration makes it harder to form CaCO₃ and can dissolve existing structures, which threatens reef-building corals (e.g. on the Great Barrier Reef), molluscs and some plankton. The other groups are affected only indirectly.

**A245.** C. 26 %
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-5 -->

Explanation: [H₃O⁺] = 10⁻ᵖᴴ, so the ratio is 10⁻⁸·¹ ÷ 10⁻⁸·² = 10⁰·¹ ≈ 1.26, an increase of about 26 %. pH is logarithmic, so a 0.1 drop is not a 1 % change.

**A246.** CO₂(g) ⇌ CO₂(aq); CO₂(aq) + H₂O(l) ⇌ H₂CO₃(aq); H₂CO₃(aq) + H₂O(l) ⇌ HCO₃⁻(aq) + H₃O⁺(aq), and the rise in [H₃O⁺] lowers the pH (some HCO₃⁻ also ionises further to CO₃²⁻ and H₃O⁺).
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-6 -->

Explanation:

**A247.** Shells form by Ca²⁺(aq) + CO₃²⁻(aq) → CaCO₃(s), which needs a good supply of carbonate ions; extra H₃O⁺ from dissolved CO₂ converts carbonate to hydrogencarbonate (H₃O⁺(aq) + CO₃²⁻(aq) → HCO₃⁻(aq) + H₂O(l)), so less CO₃²⁻ is available and shell building costs organisms more energy; at lower pH existing shells can dissolve: CaCO₃(s) + H₃O⁺(aq) → Ca²⁺(aq) + HCO₃⁻(aq) + H₂O(l).
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-7 -->

Explanation:

**A248.** The natural greenhouse effect is warming from naturally occurring greenhouse gases that keeps Earth's average temperature about 33 °C warmer than it would be otherwise; the enhanced greenhouse effect is the additional warming caused by human activities (fossil fuel combustion, land clearing, agriculture) raising greenhouse gas concentrations; methane absorbs infrared radiation far more effectively per molecule than CO₂ (a global warming potential of roughly 25–30 times CO₂ over 100 years), so emissions from livestock, landfill and gas leaks have a large effect.
<!-- id: chem12a3-go-8 -->

Explanation:

## VCE Units 3 & 4

### Fuels & thermochemistry

#### Fossil fuels and biofuels

**A249.** A. Biogas produced by anaerobic digestion of food waste
<!-- id: chem-1-1-1 -->

Explanation: Biogas comes from biomass that can be replaced within a human lifetime; the others are fossil fuels formed over millions of years.

**A250.** B. C₆H₁₂O₆(aq) → 2C₂H₅OH(aq) + 2CO₂(g)
<!-- id: chem-1-1-2 -->

Explanation: Anaerobic fermentation converts glucose into ethanol and carbon dioxide; the second equation is cellular respiration and the others are unbalanced.

**A251.** C. reacting a triglyceride with methanol in the presence of a strong base catalyst
<!-- id: chem-1-1-3 -->

Explanation: Transesterification of plant oils or animal fats with methanol, catalysed by KOH or NaOH, produces fatty acid methyl esters (biodiesel).

**A252.** Fossil fuels are burnt to power farm machinery, make fertilisers, transport the crop and heat the distillation process, so the total CO₂ released exceeds the CO₂ absorbed by the crop during photosynthesis.
<!-- id: chem-1-1-4 -->

Explanation: Award marks for identifying at least one fossil-fuel energy input in the production chain and linking it to net CO₂ emissions.

**A253.** D. Glycerol
<!-- id: chem-1-1-5 -->

Explanation: Each triglyceride releases its glycerol backbone (propane-1,2,3-triol) as the fatty acid chains are converted into methyl esters.

**A254.** A. methane
<!-- id: chem-1-1-6 -->

Explanation: Biogas is typically 50–70% methane, with most of the remainder being carbon dioxide.

**A255.** B. are generally larger and contain polar ester groups, giving stronger intermolecular forces
<!-- id: chem-1-1-7 -->

Explanation: Long-chain fatty acid methyl esters have strong dispersion forces plus dipole–dipole attractions from the ester group; esters have no O–H so cannot hydrogen bond to each other.

**A256.** Fatty acid methyl esters (biodiesel) and glycerol; the methyl esters are the biodiesel fuel.
<!-- id: chem-1-1-8 -->

Explanation: Glycerol is a by-product; both products must be named.

**A257.** C. the oxygen atoms in its ester groups can form hydrogen bonds with water molecules
<!-- id: chem-1-1-9 -->

Explanation: The lone pairs on ester oxygen atoms accept hydrogen bonds from water, making biodiesel hygroscopic; petrodiesel alkanes cannot do this.

**A258.** D. the CO₂ released when it burns is approximately equal to the CO₂ absorbed by the crop during photosynthesis
<!-- id: chem-1-1-10 -->

Explanation: In the idealised model, CO₂ taken up by photosynthesis balances the CO₂ released in fermentation and combustion.

**A259.** A. It formed from plant material compressed and heated over millions of years
<!-- id: chem-1-1-11 -->

Explanation: Coal is a fossil fuel formed from ancient plant matter under heat and pressure over geological time, so it is non-renewable.

**A260.** Biodiesel contains ester functional groups whose oxygen atoms can accept hydrogen bonds from water molecules, so it attracts and absorbs water. Petrodiesel consists of non-polar hydrocarbons that cannot hydrogen bond with water.
<!-- id: chem-1-1-12 -->

Explanation: Link the ester oxygen atoms to hydrogen bonding with water; mention that alkanes cannot.

**A261.** B. Liquefied petroleum gas (LPG)
<!-- id: chem-1-1-13 -->

Explanation: LPG (mainly propane and butane) is obtained from natural gas processing and crude oil refining, both fossil sources.

**A262.** C. Wheat straw left after harvest
<!-- id: chem-1-1-14 -->

Explanation: Crop residues like straw are lignocellulosic waste not used as human food, so using them avoids the food-versus-fuel conflict.

**A263.** D. a lower energy content per gram
<!-- id: chem-1-1-15 -->

Explanation: Biodiesel molecules are already partly oxidised (they contain oxygen), so they release less energy per gram (about 37–38 kJ g⁻¹ compared with about 45 kJ g⁻¹).

**A264.** Advantage: ethanol is renewable and can lower net greenhouse emissions (it also burns more cleanly). Disadvantage: ethanol releases less energy per gram than petrol, so fuel consumption increases (or it absorbs water, or crops compete with food).
<!-- id: chem-1-1-16 -->

Explanation: Any valid advantage and any valid disadvantage, each clearly explained.

**A265.** A. they form over millions of years, far more slowly than they are consumed
<!-- id: chem-1-1-17 -->

Explanation: Renewability depends on the rate of replenishment; crude oil cannot be replaced within a human timescale.

**A266.** B. Yeast, anaerobic conditions, about 30–40 °C
<!-- id: chem-1-1-18 -->

Explanation: Yeast enzymes ferment sugars in the absence of oxygen; temperatures that are too high denature the enzymes.

**A267.** C. fractional distillation
<!-- id: chem-1-1-19 -->

Explanation: Ethanol (b.p. 78 °C) and water (b.p. 100 °C) are miscible liquids with different boiling points, so they are separated by fractional distillation.

**A268.** A renewable fuel can be replaced by natural processes within a relatively short (human) timescale, e.g. biogas from a Gippsland dairy farm. A non-renewable fuel is used faster than it can be replaced, taking millions of years to form, e.g. Latrobe Valley brown coal.
<!-- id: chem-1-1-20 -->

Explanation: The key idea is the rate of replenishment compared with the rate of use.

**A269.** D. It contains a significant proportion of carbon dioxide, which lowers its energy content per kilogram compared with pure methane
<!-- id: chem-1-1-21 -->

Explanation: Non-combustible CO₂ makes up much of biogas, diluting the methane and reducing the energy released per kilogram of gas.

**A270.** A. 3
<!-- id: chem-1-1-22 -->

Explanation: Each of the three ester linkages reacts with one methanol molecule, forming three methyl ester molecules and one glycerol molecule.

**A271.** B. hydrocarbons such as long-chain alkanes
<!-- id: chem-1-1-23 -->

Explanation: Petrodiesel is a crude oil fraction made up largely of alkanes of about C₁₄–C₂₀; fatty acid methyl esters are biodiesel.

**A272.** The gas is biogas, mainly methane, produced by anaerobic bacteria digesting manure. The manure is continuously replaced by the cows' feed, which is regrown each season, so the fuel source is replenished within a short timescale.
<!-- id: chem-1-1-24 -->

Explanation: Must identify biomass origin and rapid replenishment.

**A273.** Ethanol has a polar O–H group that forms hydrogen bonds with water molecules, so it is miscible. Octane is non-polar and has only dispersion forces, which cannot compete with the hydrogen bonding between water molecules, so it does not dissolve.
<!-- id: chem-1-1-25 -->

Explanation: Refer to hydrogen bonding for ethanol and dispersion forces only for octane.

**A274.** C. The plant material used to make bioethanol can be regrown on a human timescale, whereas petrol comes from crude oil formed over millions of years.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ffb-1 -->

Explanation: A renewable resource is replenished at a rate comparable to its use; crops regrow within a season or a few years. Bioethanol still releases CO₂ when it burns, and its energy content per gram (about 29.6 kJ g⁻¹) is lower than that of petrol.

**A275.** B. transesterification.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ffb-2 -->

Explanation: Triglycerides react with methanol to form fatty acid methyl esters (biodiesel) and glycerol; swapping the alcohol part of an ester is transesterification. Fermentation produces ethanol and anaerobic digestion produces biogas.

**A276.** A. methane.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ffb-3 -->

Explanation: Anaerobic digestion of organic waste produces biogas, which is mostly methane (with CO₂). Ethanol comes from yeast fermentation of sugars, not from digestion of manure.

**A277.** B. contain ester groups that add dipole–dipole attractions between molecules.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ffb-4 -->

Explanation: The ester functional group is polar, so biodiesel molecules experience dipole–dipole attractions in addition to dispersion forces, which raises viscosity and cloud point. Esters have no O–H group, so they cannot hydrogen bond with each other.

**A278.** D. Bioethanol absorbs water from the air, which can cause corrosion and phase separation in fuel tanks.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ffb-5 -->

Explanation: Ethanol is hygroscopic because its hydroxyl group hydrogen bonds with water, which can lead to corrosion and separation in blends. Ethanol actually burns more cleanly than petrol and is commonly blended with it as E10.

**A279.** C₆H₁₂O₆(aq) → 2C₂H₅OH(aq) + 2CO₂(g); the reaction is catalysed by enzymes in yeast (anaerobic conditions).
<!-- id: chem34a1-ffb-6 -->

Explanation:
