**A280.** The CO₂ released when a biofuel burns was absorbed from the atmosphere by the plants during photosynthesis, so in theory there is no net increase in atmospheric CO₂; however, fossil fuels are used in farming, fertiliser production, harvesting, transport and processing (e.g. distillation), so there is a net release of CO₂; land clearing for crops can also release CO₂.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ffb-7 -->

Explanation:

**A281.** They formed from the remains of ancient plants and marine organisms buried and subjected to heat and pressure over millions of years; they are used far faster than they are formed, so supplies cannot be replenished on a human timescale.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ffb-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Combustion and thermochemical equations

**A282.** A. CH₄(g) + 2O₂(g) → CO₂(g) + 2H₂O(l)
<!-- id: chem-1-2-1 -->

Explanation: Complete combustion in excess oxygen produces only carbon dioxide and water; the other equations show incomplete combustion or impossible products.

**A283.** B. 25
<!-- id: chem-1-2-2 -->

Explanation: Oxygen atoms on the right: 16 × 2 + 18 × 1 = 50, so 25 O₂ molecules are needed.

**A284.** C. carbon monoxide, soot and water
<!-- id: chem-1-2-3 -->

Explanation: With limited oxygen, carbon is only partly oxidised, forming CO and/or elemental carbon (soot) along with water.

**A285.** C₂H₅OH(l) + 3O₂(g) → 2CO₂(g) + 3H₂O(l)   ΔH = −1364 kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-1-2-4 -->

Explanation: Check balancing (O: 1 + 6 = 4 + 3), states, the negative sign and units.

**A286.** D. binds strongly to haemoglobin, reducing oxygen transport in the blood
<!-- id: chem-1-2-5 -->

Explanation: CO binds to haemoglobin far more strongly than O₂, starving tissues of oxygen; it is also colourless and odourless.

**A287.** A. −1780 kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-1-2-6 -->

Explanation: Doubling all coefficients doubles the enthalpy change: 2 × (−890) = −1780 kJ mol⁻¹.

**A288.** B. +890 kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-1-2-7 -->

Explanation: Reversing a reaction reverses the sign of ΔH but keeps the magnitude.

**A289.** 2H₂(g) + O₂(g) → 2H₂O(l)   ΔH = −572 kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-1-2-8 -->

Explanation: ΔH must be doubled to match the 2 mol of H₂ in the equation.

**A290.** C. The products have lower enthalpy than the reactants and ΔH is negative
<!-- id: chem-1-2-9 -->

Explanation: Energy is released to the surroundings, so H(products) < H(reactants) and ΔH = H(products) − H(reactants) < 0.

**A291.** D. Photosynthesis
<!-- id: chem-1-2-10 -->

Explanation: Photosynthesis absorbs light energy to build glucose from CO₂ and water; the others release energy.

**A292.** A. 2C₄H₁₀(g) + 13O₂(g) → 8CO₂(g) + 10H₂O(l)   ΔH = −5752 kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-1-2-11 -->

Explanation: The equation shows 2 mol of butane, so ΔH = 2 × (−2876) = −5752 kJ mol⁻¹; combustion is exothermic.

**A293.** ΔH = H(products) − H(reactants). In an exothermic reaction energy is released to the surroundings, so the products have less enthalpy than the reactants and ΔH is negative.
<!-- id: chem-1-2-12 -->

Explanation: Refer to the definition of ΔH and energy released to the surroundings.

**A294.** B. greater than the activation energy of the forward reaction
<!-- id: chem-1-2-13 -->

Explanation: Ea(reverse) = Ea(forward) + |ΔH|, so for an exothermic reaction the reverse barrier is larger.

**A295.** C. 200 kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-1-2-14 -->

Explanation: Ea(reverse) = Ea(forward) − ΔH = 120 − (−80) = 200 kJ mol⁻¹.

**A296.** D. 20 kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-1-2-15 -->

Explanation: Ea(reverse) = Ea(forward) − ΔH = 50 − 30 = 20 kJ mol⁻¹.

**A297.** ΔH = +4440 kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-1-2-16 -->

Explanation: The reaction is reversed (sign changes) and doubled: −(2 × −2220) = +4440 kJ mol⁻¹.

**A298.** A. condensing water vapour to liquid releases additional energy
<!-- id: chem-1-2-17 -->

Explanation: H₂O(g) → H₂O(l) is exothermic, so forming liquid water releases extra energy, making ΔH more negative.

**A299.** B. The energy absorbed breaking bonds in the reactants is less than the energy released forming bonds in the products
<!-- id: chem-1-2-18 -->

Explanation: Bond breaking is endothermic and bond forming is exothermic; combustion is exothermic overall because more energy is released than absorbed.

**A300.** C. 10 mol
<!-- id: chem-1-2-19 -->

Explanation: The mole ratio O₂ : C₃H₈ is 5 : 1, so 2.0 × 5 = 10 mol of O₂.

**A301.** With limited oxygen, incomplete combustion occurs. Products include carbon monoxide, carbon (soot) and water, along with less carbon dioxide; less energy is released per mole of fuel.
<!-- id: chem-1-2-20 -->

Explanation: Must name incomplete combustion and CO and/or soot as products.

**A302.** D. 132 g
<!-- id: chem-1-2-21 -->

Explanation: 1 mol C₃H₈ produces 3 mol CO₂; 3 × 44.0 = 132 g.

**A303.** A. 49.6 L
<!-- id: chem-1-2-22 -->

Explanation: 0.500 mol C₄H₁₀ produces 4 × 0.500 = 2.00 mol CO₂; 2.00 × 24.8 = 49.6 L.

**A304.** B. 2CH₄(g) + 3O₂(g) → 2CO(g) + 4H₂O(l)
<!-- id: chem-1-2-23 -->

Explanation: Checking atoms: 2 C, 8 H and 6 O on each side; each of the other equations has unequal numbers of oxygen atoms.

**A305.** ΔH = −890 + 2(+44) = −802 kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-1-2-24 -->

Explanation: Two moles of water must be vaporised, absorbing 88 kJ, so less energy is released overall.

**A306.** C₅H₁₂(l) + 8O₂(g) → 5CO₂(g) + 6H₂O(l); the mole ratio CO₂ : C₅H₁₂ is 5 : 1.
<!-- id: chem-1-2-25 -->

Explanation: Check: O atoms on the right = 10 + 6 = 16 = 8 O₂.

**A307.** A. C₃H₈(g) + 5O₂(g) → 3CO₂(g) + 4H₂O(l)  ΔH = −2220 kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem34a1-cte-1 -->

Explanation: Complete combustion forms CO₂ and, at SLC, liquid water; combustion is exothermic so ΔH is negative. Doubling the coefficients would require ΔH to double to −4440 kJ mol⁻¹.

**A308.** C. −5464 kJ
<!-- id: chem34a1-cte-2 -->

Explanation: The ΔH value applies to the mole amounts in the equation as written (2 mol octane), so 1 mol releases half: −10 928 ÷ 2 = −5464 kJ.

**A309.** B. releases less energy per mole of methane and can produce carbon monoxide and soot.
<!-- id: chem34a1-cte-3 -->

Explanation: In incomplete combustion carbon is only partially oxidised to CO or C, so less energy is released per mole of fuel, and toxic CO and soot are produced.

**A310.** D. +572 kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem34a1-cte-4 -->

Explanation: Reversing the equation changes the sign (+286) and doubling the coefficients doubles the value: +572 kJ mol⁻¹.

**A311.** A. C₂H₅OH(l) + 2O₂(g) → 2CO(g) + 3H₂O(l)
<!-- id: chem34a1-cte-5 -->

Explanation: Products contain 2 + 3 = 5 O atoms; ethanol supplies 1, so 4 O atoms (2 O₂) are needed. Option D is not balanced for oxygen (7 O atoms on the right).

**A312.** 2C₄H₁₀(g) + 13O₂(g) → 8CO₂(g) + 10H₂O(l) ΔH = −5748 kJ mol⁻¹ (or C₄H₁₀(g) + 13/2 O₂(g) → 4CO₂(g) + 5H₂O(l) ΔH = −2874 kJ mol⁻¹); correct formulas, balancing and states; ΔH sign and value consistent with the coefficients.
<!-- id: chem34a1-cte-6 -->

Explanation:

**A313.** n(CH₃OH) = 80.0 ÷ 32.0 = 2.50 mol; energy = 2.50 × 726 = 1815 kJ ≈ 1.82 × 10³ kJ released.
<!-- id: chem34a1-cte-7 -->

Explanation:

**A314.** At SLC (25 °C, 100 kPa) water is a liquid, so the standard value refers to liquid water; if water were formed as a gas, less energy would be released because energy is absorbed when water vaporises, so |ΔH| would be smaller (less negative).
<!-- id: chem34a1-cte-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Enthalpy and fuel energy calculations

**A315.** A. 1780 kJ
<!-- id: chem-1-3-1 -->

Explanation: n = 32.0 / 16.0 = 2.00 mol; energy = 2.00 × 890 = 1780 kJ.

**A316.** B. 29.7 kJ g⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-1-3-2 -->

Explanation: 1364 / 46.0 = 29.7 kJ g⁻¹.

**A317.** C. 47.9 MJ
<!-- id: chem-1-3-3 -->

Explanation: n = 1000 / 114.0 = 8.77 mol; energy = 8.77 × 5464 = 4.79 × 10⁴ kJ = 47.9 MJ.

**A318.** 2220 / 44.0 = 50.5 kJ g⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-1-3-4 -->

Explanation: Divide molar enthalpy by molar mass; give 3 significant figures.

**A319.** D. 40.0 g
<!-- id: chem-1-3-5 -->

Explanation: n = 2225 / 890 = 2.50 mol; m = 2.50 × 16.0 = 40.0 g.

**A320.** A. 12.4 L
<!-- id: chem-1-3-6 -->

Explanation: n = 445 / 890 = 0.500 mol; V = 0.500 × 24.8 = 12.4 L.

**A321.** B. 895 kJ
<!-- id: chem-1-3-7 -->

Explanation: n = 10.0 / 24.8 = 0.403 mol; energy = 0.403 × 2220 = 895 kJ (991 kJ would result from wrongly using 22.4 L mol⁻¹).

**A322.** n(C₂H₅OH) = 1000 / 1364 = 0.733 mol; n(CO₂) = 2 × 0.733 = 1.47 mol; m = 1.47 × 44.0 = 64.5 g
<!-- id: chem-1-3-8 -->

Explanation: Remember the 2 : 1 mole ratio of CO₂ to ethanol.

**A323.** C. Hydrogen
<!-- id: chem-1-3-9 -->

Explanation: Per gram: H₂ 286/2.0 = 143; CH₄ 890/16.0 = 55.6; C₂H₅OH 1364/46.0 = 29.7; C₈H₁₈ 5464/114.0 = 47.9 kJ g⁻¹.

**A324.** D. Methane
<!-- id: chem-1-3-10 -->

Explanation: Energy per mol CO₂: CH₄ 890/1 = 890; C₃H₈ 2220/3 = 740; C₂H₅OH 1364/2 = 682; C₈H₁₈ 5464/8 = 683 kJ.

**A325.** A. 148 kJ
<!-- id: chem-1-3-11 -->

Explanation: n = 5.00 / 46.0 = 0.109 mol; energy = 0.109 × 1364 = 148 kJ.

**A326.** n = 10 000 / 890 = 11.2 mol; V = 11.2 × 24.8 = 279 L
<!-- id: chem-1-3-12 -->

Explanation: Convert MJ to kJ first: 10.0 MJ = 10 000 kJ.

**A327.** B. −2.88 × 10³ kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-1-3-13 -->

Explanation: 49.6 kJ g⁻¹ × 58.0 g mol⁻¹ = 2877 kJ mol⁻¹, released, so ΔH = −2.88 × 10³ kJ mol⁻¹.

**A328.** C. 1.51 × 10⁶ kJ
<!-- id: chem-1-3-14 -->

Explanation: m = 45 000 mL × 0.700 = 31 500 g; n = 31 500 / 114.0 = 276 mol; energy = 276 × 5464 = 1.51 × 10⁶ kJ.

**A329.** D. 197 kJ
<!-- id: chem-1-3-15 -->

Explanation: n = 6.0 / 12.0 = 0.50 mol; energy = 0.50 × 394 = 197 kJ.

**A330.** n = 9000 / 44.0 = 205 mol; energy = 205 × 2220 = 4.5 × 10⁵ kJ = 4.5 × 10² MJ (about 454 MJ)
<!-- id: chem-1-3-16 -->

Explanation: Convert kg to g and kJ to MJ; 2 significant figures matches the 9.0 kg data.

**A331.** A. 3.28 × 10⁷ kJ
<!-- id: chem-1-3-17 -->

Explanation: 1.00 tonne = 1.00 × 10⁶ g; n = 10⁶ / 12.0 = 8.33 × 10⁴ mol; energy = 8.33 × 10⁴ × 394 = 3.28 × 10⁷ kJ.

**A332.** B. 49.4 g
<!-- id: chem-1-3-18 -->

Explanation: n(CH₄) = 1000 / 890 = 1.12 mol, giving 1.12 mol CO₂; m = 1.12 × 44.0 = 49.4 g.

**A333.** C. 1.37 × 10³ kJ
<!-- id: chem-1-3-19 -->

Explanation: Energy = 0.250 × 5464 = 1366 kJ ≈ 1.37 × 10³ kJ.

**A334.** Energy from 1.00 g octane = (1.00 / 114.0) × 5464 = 47.9 kJ; ethanol releases 1364 / 46.0 = 29.7 kJ g⁻¹, so mass = 47.9 / 29.7 = 1.62 g
<!-- id: chem-1-3-20 -->

Explanation: Shows why E10 vehicles use slightly more fuel per kilometre.

**A335.** D. −5464 kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-1-3-21 -->

Explanation: ΔH in the equation is for 2 mol of octane, so per mole it is −10 928 / 2 = −5464 kJ mol⁻¹.

**A336.** A. 2.15 × 10³ kJ
<!-- id: chem-1-3-22 -->

Explanation: V(CH₄) = 60.0 L; n = 60.0 / 24.8 = 2.42 mol; energy = 2.42 × 890 = 2.15 × 10³ kJ (CO₂ does not burn).

**A337.** B. 2.00 kg
<!-- id: chem-1-3-23 -->

Explanation: n = 2.86 × 10⁵ / 286 = 1000 mol; m = 1000 × 2.0 = 2000 g = 2.00 kg.

**A338.** n = 1000 / 286 = 3.50 mol; m = 3.50 × 2.0 = 6.99 g (about 7.0 g)
<!-- id: chem-1-3-24 -->

Explanation: Use M(H₂) = 2.0 g mol⁻¹, not 1.0.

**A339.** Vehicles carry fuel by mass (and volume), so the energy per gram tells how much energy is available for a given mass of fuel carried. Molar values ignore that molecules of different fuels have very different masses.
<!-- id: chem-1-3-25 -->

Explanation: Link to the practical limitation of fuel mass and storage in vehicles.

**A340.** B. the products are at a lower enthalpy than the reactants and ΔH is negative.
<!-- id: chem34a1-eep-1 -->

Explanation: An exothermic reaction releases energy to the surroundings, so the products have lower enthalpy and ΔH < 0. All reactions, including exothermic ones, have an activation energy.

**A341.** A. 180 kJ mol⁻¹ and +90 kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem34a1-eep-2 -->

Explanation: Ea = 230 − 50 = 180 kJ mol⁻¹; ΔH = H(products) − H(reactants) = 140 − 50 = +90 kJ mol⁻¹, so the reaction is endothermic.

**A342.** C. 90 kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem34a1-eep-3 -->

Explanation: The reverse reaction starts from the products: Ea(reverse) = 230 − 140 = 90 kJ mol⁻¹.

**A343.** D. The process is endothermic; the system absorbs energy from the surroundings, so ΔH is positive.
<!-- id: chem34a1-eep-4 -->

Explanation: The temperature of the surroundings (the water and your hand) falls because the dissolving process absorbs energy, so it is endothermic with ΔH > 0.

**A344.** B. endothermic and exothermic.
<!-- id: chem34a1-eep-5 -->

Explanation: Energy must be absorbed to break bonds (endothermic) and is released when new bonds form (exothermic). An overall reaction is exothermic when more energy is released forming bonds than absorbed breaking them.

**A345.** Axes: enthalpy (vertical) against reaction progress (horizontal); reactants CH₄(g) + 2O₂(g) on a higher level, products CO₂(g) + 2H₂O(l) 890 kJ mol⁻¹ lower, with a hump between labelled transition state; Ea arrow from reactants to top of hump (250 kJ mol⁻¹) and ΔH arrow from reactants down to products; Ea(reverse) = 250 + 890 = 1140 kJ mol⁻¹.
<!-- id: chem34a1-eep-6 -->

Explanation:

**A346.** The reaction has a high activation energy, so at room temperature very few collisions between CH₄ and O₂ have enough energy to break the reactant bonds; a spark or flame provides enough energy to overcome Ea, after which the energy released sustains the reaction.
<!-- id: chem34a1-eep-7 -->

Explanation:

**A347.** Energy absorbed breaking bonds = 2(436) + 498 = 1370 kJ; energy released forming bonds = 4 × 463 = 1852 kJ; ΔH = 1370 − 1852 = −482 kJ mol⁻¹ (exothermic).
<!-- id: chem34a1-eep-8 -->

Explanation:

**A348.** B. 16.9 L
<!-- id: chem34a1-gse-1 -->

Explanation: n(C₃H₈) = 10.0 ÷ 44.0 = 0.227 mol; C₃H₈ : CO₂ = 1 : 3, so n(CO₂) = 0.682 mol; V = 0.682 × 24.8 = 16.9 L.

**A349.** D. 1.79 × 10³ kJ
<!-- id: chem34a1-gse-2 -->

Explanation: n(CH₄) = 50.0 ÷ 24.8 = 2.02 mol; energy = 2.02 × 890 = 1.79 × 10³ kJ.

**A350.** A. 0.117 mol
<!-- id: chem34a1-gse-3 -->

Explanation: n = PV ÷ RT = (150 × 2.00) ÷ (8.31 × 308) = 0.117 mol. Temperature must be in kelvin (35 + 273 = 308 K); using 35 gives 1.03 mol.

**A351.** C. 21.7 L
<!-- id: chem34a1-gse-4 -->

Explanation: For gases at the same temperature and pressure, volume ratios equal mole ratios: V(O₂) = 6.20 × 7/2 = 21.7 L.

**A352.** C. 1.62 kg
<!-- id: chem34a1-gse-5 -->

Explanation: Energy from octane = 1000 × 47.9 = 47 900 kJ; mass of ethanol = 47 900 ÷ 29.6 = 1618 g ≈ 1.62 kg.

**A353.** n(C₃H₈) = 450 ÷ 44.0 = 10.2 mol; (a) energy = 10.2 × 2220 = 2.27 × 10⁴ kJ; (b) n(CO₂) = 3 × 10.2 = 30.7 mol, V = 30.7 × 24.8 = 761 L (7.61 × 10² L).
<!-- id: chem34a1-gse-6 -->

Explanation:

**A354.** n(CH₄) = 5.00 ÷ 24.8 = 0.202 mol; energy released = 0.202 × 890 = 179 kJ; energy transferred = 0.650 × 179 = 117 kJ.
<!-- id: chem34a1-gse-7 -->

Explanation:

**A355.** H₂: (1 ÷ 24.8) × 286 = 11.5 kJ L⁻¹; CH₄: (1 ÷ 24.8) × 890 = 35.9 kJ L⁻¹; hydrogen releases about one-third the energy per litre, so it must be compressed to high pressure or liquefied at very low temperature to store useful amounts, which costs energy and needs strong tanks.
<!-- id: chem34a1-gse-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Calorimetry and specific heat capacity

**A356.** A. 62.7 kJ
<!-- id: chem-1-4-1 -->

Explanation: q = mcΔT = 250 × 4.18 × 60.0 = 62 700 J = 62.7 kJ.

**A357.** B. raise the temperature of 1 g of the substance by 1 °C
<!-- id: chem-1-4-2 -->

Explanation: Specific heat capacity is measured per gram per degree, in J g⁻¹ °C⁻¹; the energy per degree for the whole calorimeter is the calibration factor.

**A358.** C. extensive hydrogen bonding between molecules absorbs energy before the molecules move faster
<!-- id: chem-1-4-3 -->

Explanation: Much of the added energy goes into disrupting hydrogen bonds rather than increasing the kinetic energy of the molecules, so temperature rises slowly.

**A359.** E = VIt = 5.00 × 3.00 × 120 = 1800 J; CF = 1800 / 0.750 = 2.40 × 10³ J °C⁻¹ (2.40 kJ °C⁻¹)
<!-- id: chem-1-4-4 -->

Explanation: Include units; time must be in seconds.

**A360.** D. 2.00 × 10³ J °C⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-1-4-5 -->

Explanation: E = VIt = 6.00 × 2.50 × 180 = 2700 J; CF = 2700 / 1.35 = 2.00 × 10³ J °C⁻¹.

**A361.** A. 14.7 kJ
<!-- id: chem-1-4-6 -->

Explanation: q = CF × ΔT = 3.50 × 4.20 = 14.7 kJ.

**A362.** B. −1.00 × 10³ kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-1-4-7 -->

Explanation: q = 200 × 4.18 × 12.0 = 10 032 J = 10.0 kJ; n = 0.460 / 46.0 = 0.0100 mol; ΔH = −10.0 / 0.0100 = −1.00 × 10³ kJ mol⁻¹.

**A363.** q = 4.25 × 6.80 = 28.9 kJ; n = 1.15 / 46.0 = 0.0250 mol; ΔH = −28.9 / 0.0250 = −1.16 × 10³ kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-1-4-8 -->

Explanation: Negative sign required as combustion is exothermic.

**A364.** C. significant heat is lost to the surroundings rather than absorbed by the water
<!-- id: chem-1-4-9 -->

Explanation: In open spirit-burner setups much of the heat escapes to the air and the can, and combustion may be incomplete, so less energy is measured.

**A365.** D. Using a draught shield and insulating lid around the can
<!-- id: chem-1-4-10 -->

Explanation: A draught shield and lid reduce heat loss to the surroundings, so more of the energy released is transferred to the water.

**A366.** A. +25.9 kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-1-4-11 -->

Explanation: q = 100 × 4.18 × 3.10 = 1296 J; n = 4.00 / 80.0 = 0.0500 mol; ΔH = +1.296 / 0.0500 = +25.9 kJ mol⁻¹ (positive because temperature fell).

**A367.** q = mcΔT accounts only for energy absorbed by the water, whereas the calibration factor includes the energy absorbed by the container, stirrer, thermometer and any other parts of the calorimeter, as well as typical heat losses under the same conditions.
<!-- id: chem-1-4-12 -->

Explanation: Key idea: the calibration factor measures the whole system, not just the water.

**A368.** B. 2.39 °C
<!-- id: chem-1-4-13 -->

Explanation: ΔT = q / (mc) = 5000 / (500 × 4.18) = 2.39 °C.

**A369.** C. The ethanol has the larger temperature increase
<!-- id: chem-1-4-14 -->

Explanation: ΔT = q / (mc), so the liquid with the smaller specific heat capacity (ethanol) has the larger temperature rise.

**A370.** D. burns the sample in a sealed steel vessel with excess oxygen
<!-- id: chem-1-4-15 -->

Explanation: The sealed, oxygen-filled 'bomb' ensures complete combustion and minimal heat loss; it still requires calibration.

**A371.** Heat lost to the surroundings and the metal can, and incomplete combustion (soot on the can). Both mean less energy reaches the water, so the calculated heat of combustion is lower than the true value. Evaporation of ethanol from the wick before weighing would also make the calculated value too low.
<!-- id: chem-1-4-16 -->

Explanation: Any two valid errors, each with its effect (value too low).

**A372.** A. determine how much energy is needed to raise the temperature of the whole calorimeter and its contents by 1 °C
<!-- id: chem-1-4-17 -->

Explanation: The calibration factor accounts for energy absorbed by the water, container, stirrer and thermometer together.

**A373.** B. 0.450 J g⁻¹ °C⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-1-4-18 -->

Explanation: c = q / (mΔT) = 450 / (50.0 × 20.0) = 0.450 J g⁻¹ °C⁻¹.

**A374.** C. −725 kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-1-4-19 -->

Explanation: q = 9.80 × 0.740 = 7.25 kJ; n = 0.320 / 32.0 = 0.0100 mol; ΔH = −7.25 / 0.0100 = −725 kJ mol⁻¹.

**A375.** q = 100 × 4.18 × 6.70 = 2.80 × 10³ J = 2.80 kJ; n(H₂O) = 0.0500 mol; ΔH = −2.80 / 0.0500 = −56.0 kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-1-4-20 -->

Explanation: n(HCl) = 1.00 × 0.0500 = 0.0500 mol; temperature rise means ΔH is negative.

**A376.** D. 5.02 kJ g⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-1-4-21 -->

Explanation: q = 100 × 4.18 × 24.0 = 10 032 J = 10.0 kJ; per gram = 10.0 / 2.00 = 5.02 kJ g⁻¹.

**A377.** A. The reaction is exothermic and ΔH is negative
<!-- id: chem-1-4-22 -->

Explanation: A temperature rise means energy was released by the reaction into the solution, so the reaction is exothermic with ΔH < 0.

**A378.** B. 60.1%
<!-- id: chem-1-4-23 -->

Explanation: Efficiency = 820 / 1364 × 100 = 60.1%.

**A379.** Energy released = (0.200 / 16.0) × 890 = 11.1 kJ; absorbed = 0.700 × 11.1 = 7.79 kJ; ΔT = 7790 / (300 × 4.18) = 6.21 °C
<!-- id: chem-1-4-24 -->

Explanation: Apply the efficiency before using ΔT = q / (mc).

**A380.** The lid reduces heat loss (or gain) to the surroundings by convection and evaporation. The stirrer ensures the solution has a uniform temperature so the thermometer reads the true temperature change.
<!-- id: chem-1-4-25 -->

Explanation: One clear purpose for each piece of equipment.

**A381.** B. 1440 J °C⁻¹
<!-- id: chem34a1-cal-1 -->

Explanation: E = VIt = 6.00 × 2.50 × 120 = 1800 J; CF = E ÷ ΔT = 1800 ÷ 1.25 = 1440 J °C⁻¹ (1.44 kJ °C⁻¹). 1800 J is the energy, not the calibration factor.

**A382.** A. It accounts for energy absorbed by the calorimeter itself (container, thermometer, stirrer) and some heat loss.
<!-- id: chem34a1-cal-2 -->

Explanation: The calibration factor measures the energy needed to raise the temperature of the whole calorimeter and its contents by 1 °C, so it includes the apparatus, not just the water.

**A383.** C. −1.07 × 10³ kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem34a1-cal-3 -->

Explanation: q = 150.0 × 4.18 × 18.5 = 11.6 kJ; n = 0.500 ÷ 46.0 = 0.01087 mol; ΔH = −11.6 ÷ 0.01087 = −1.07 × 10³ kJ mol⁻¹. −1364 kJ mol⁻¹ is the data book value; the experimental value is smaller because of heat loss.

**A384.** C. Heat was lost to the surroundings and the can, and some combustion was incomplete.
<!-- id: chem34a1-cal-4 -->

Explanation: Spirit burner calorimeters are poorly insulated, so much of the energy heats the air and the can rather than the water; soot formation shows incomplete combustion. These are systematic errors that always lower the result.

**A385.** A. 41.7
<!-- id: chem34a1-cal-5 -->

Explanation: Energy = 8.62 × 5.80 = 50.0 kJ; per gram = 50.0 ÷ 1.20 = 41.7 kJ g⁻¹.

**A386.** Energy released = 420 × 13.6 = 5712 J = 5.71 kJ; n(H₂O) = n(H⁺) = 0.1000 L × 1.00 M = 0.100 mol; ΔH = −5.71 ÷ 0.100 = −57.1 kJ mol⁻¹ (negative sign because exothermic).
<!-- id: chem34a1-cal-6 -->

Explanation:

**A387.** The calibration factor is the energy needed to raise the temperature of the calorimeter and its contents by 1 °C; changing the volume of water changes the total heat capacity, so the old calibration factor no longer applies.
<!-- id: chem34a1-cal-7 -->

Explanation:

**A388.** Extrapolate the cooling section of the graph back to the time the reaction (or heating) began and take ΔT as the difference between this extrapolated temperature and the initial temperature; this corrects for heat lost to the surroundings while the temperature was rising.
<!-- id: chem34a1-cal-8 -->

Explanation:

### Galvanic & fuel cells

#### Electrochemical series

**A389.** D. Ag⁺(aq)
<!-- id: chem34a1-ecs-1 -->

Explanation: The strongest oxidising agent is the species on the left-hand side with the highest E° value. Ag⁺/Ag (+0.80 V) is higher than Cu²⁺/Cu (+0.34 V). Zn(s) is a reducing agent.

**A390.** B. Pb²⁺(aq)
<!-- id: chem34a1-ecs-2 -->

Explanation: A reaction occurs when the oxidising agent is higher on the series than the reducing agent. Pb²⁺/Pb (−0.13 V) is above Ni²⁺/Ni (−0.25 V), so Ni reduces Pb²⁺. Zn²⁺, Mg²⁺ and Al³⁺ are all below Ni²⁺.

**A391.** A. Copper
<!-- id: chem34a1-ecs-3 -->

Explanation: Cu²⁺/Cu (+0.34 V) lies above 2H⁺/H₂ (0.00 V), so H⁺ cannot oxidise copper. Iron, zinc and magnesium all lie below H⁺ and release hydrogen gas.

**A392.** B. the series predicts only whether a reaction is thermodynamically possible, not its rate; conditions may also differ from standard.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ecs-4 -->

Explanation: The electrochemical series is based on standard conditions and says nothing about rate; a predicted reaction may be too slow to observe, and non-standard concentrations or temperatures shift potentials.

**A393.** A. Fe²⁺(aq) + 2e⁻ → Fe(s) and Fe²⁺(aq) → Fe³⁺(aq) + e⁻
<!-- id: chem34a1-ecs-5 -->

Explanation: As an oxidising agent Fe²⁺ gains electrons (reduced to Fe); as a reducing agent it loses an electron (oxidised to Fe³⁺). The other options do not show Fe²⁺ itself in both roles.

**A394.** Blue colour fades and a brown/pink solid (copper) forms; zinc dissolves and the mixture warms; oxidation: Zn(s) → Zn²⁺(aq) + 2e⁻; reduction: Cu²⁺(aq) + 2e⁻ → Cu(s); overall: Zn(s) + Cu²⁺(aq) → Zn²⁺(aq) + Cu(s).
<!-- id: chem34a1-ecs-6 -->

Explanation:

**A395.** Y < X < Z; X reduces Y²⁺ so X is a stronger reducing agent than Y; Z reduces X²⁺ so Z is stronger than X; consistent with Y not reducing Z²⁺.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ecs-7 -->

Explanation:

**A396.** Br₂ is above I⁻ in the series (+1.09 V > +0.54 V), so Br₂ is a stronger oxidising agent than I₂ and reacts: Br₂(aq) + 2I⁻(aq) → 2Br⁻(aq) + I₂(aq); Br₂ is below Cl₂ (+1.09 V < +1.36 V), so it is not strong enough to oxidise Cl⁻.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ecs-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Galvanic cells

**A397.** D. copper, 1.10 V
<!-- id: chem34a1-gc-1 -->

Explanation: Cu²⁺ is the stronger oxidising agent and is reduced at the copper electrode, so copper is the cathode; E°cell = 0.34 − (−0.76) = 1.10 V.

**A398.** B. from the anode to the cathode through the external circuit.
<!-- id: chem34a1-gc-2 -->

Explanation: Oxidation at the anode releases electrons, which flow through the wire to the cathode. The salt bridge carries ions, never electrons.

**A399.** A. K⁺ ions move toward the cathode half-cell and NO₃⁻ ions move toward the anode half-cell.
<!-- id: chem34a1-gc-3 -->

Explanation: At the cathode positive ions are consumed (or negative ions formed), so cations migrate there to maintain electrical neutrality; anions move toward the anode where positive ions are being produced.

**A400.** C. 2Ag⁺(aq) + Ni(s) → 2Ag(s) + Ni²⁺(aq)
<!-- id: chem34a1-gc-4 -->

Explanation: Ag⁺ (+0.80 V) is reduced and Ni (−0.25 V) is oxidised; balancing electrons requires 2Ag⁺. Option B is not balanced for charge.

**A401.** D. conditions were non-standard, e.g. the solutions were not 1.0 M or the temperature was not 25 °C.
<!-- id: chem34a1-gc-5 -->

Explanation: E° values apply only under standard conditions (1 M, 25 °C, 100 kPa); real measurements differ because of concentration, temperature and internal resistance. The predicted value is −0.13 − (−0.44) = 0.31 V.

**A402.** Anode: magnesium, negative electrode; anode: Mg(s) → Mg²⁺(aq) + 2e⁻; cathode (carbon, positive): Fe³⁺(aq) + e⁻ → Fe²⁺(aq); E°cell = 0.77 − (−2.37) = 3.14 V.
<!-- id: chem34a1-gc-6 -->

Explanation:

**A403.** If mixed, electrons would transfer directly between the reactants and the chemical energy would be released as heat; separating them forces electrons to travel through an external circuit, so chemical energy is converted into electrical energy.
<!-- id: chem34a1-gc-7 -->

Explanation:

**A404.** The zinc anode loses mass because Zn is oxidised to Zn²⁺ which goes into solution; the copper cathode gains mass because Cu²⁺ is reduced and deposited as Cu; the blue colour of the Cu²⁺ solution fades as Cu²⁺ concentration decreases.
<!-- id: chem34a1-gc-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Fuel cells

**A405.** C. O₂(g) + 4H⁺(aq) + 4e⁻ → 2H₂O(l)
<!-- id: chem34a1-fc-1 -->

Explanation: Oxygen is reduced at the cathode; in acid, H⁺ is available, so O₂ + 4H⁺ + 4e⁻ → 2H₂O. Option B applies to an alkaline electrolyte, and option A is the anode reaction.

**A406.** D. Reactants are supplied continuously from outside the cell, so it does not run flat while fuel is supplied.
<!-- id: chem34a1-fc-2 -->

Explanation: Both types are galvanic and convert chemical to electrical energy via redox, but fuel cells receive a continuous external supply of fuel and oxidant, whereas a battery contains a fixed amount of reactants.

**A407.** A. H₂(g) + 2OH⁻(aq) → 2H₂O(l) + 2e⁻
<!-- id: chem34a1-fc-3 -->

Explanation: Hydrogen is oxidised at the anode; in an alkaline electrolyte, OH⁻ ions combine with the H⁺ formed to give water. Option D is a reduction.

**A408.** A. increase the surface area for reaction and lower the activation energy, increasing the rate of the electrode reactions.
<!-- id: chem34a1-fc-4 -->

Explanation: Porous electrodes allow gases, electrolyte and electrode to meet over a large surface area, and catalysts speed up the reactions. Catalysts do not change the thermodynamic cell voltage.

**A409.** B. CH₃OH(l) + H₂O(l) → CO₂(g) + 6H⁺(aq) + 6e⁻
<!-- id: chem34a1-fc-5 -->

Explanation: Methanol is oxidised at the anode. Balance C, then O with H₂O, then H with H⁺, then charge with electrons: CH₃OH + H₂O → CO₂ + 6H⁺ + 6e⁻. Option D is not balanced for O or H.

**A410.** Advantages: higher energy efficiency because chemical energy converts directly to electrical energy (avoiding heat → mechanical → electrical steps); only product is water, so no CO₂ or other pollutants at point of use; quiet with no moving parts; disadvantage: hydrogen is expensive and difficult to store/transport, catalysts such as platinum are costly, or most hydrogen is currently made from fossil fuels.
<!-- id: chem34a1-fc-6 -->

Explanation:

**A411.** Anode: CH₄(g) + 2H₂O(l) → CO₂(g) + 8H⁺(aq) + 8e⁻; cathode: O₂(g) + 4H⁺(aq) + 4e⁻ → 2H₂O(l) (×2); overall: CH₄(g) + 2O₂(g) → CO₂(g) + 2H₂O(l).
<!-- id: chem34a1-fc-7 -->

Explanation:

**A412.** H⁺ ions are produced at the anode (H₂ → 2H⁺ + 2e⁻) and consumed at the cathode (O₂ + 4H⁺ + 4e⁻ → 2H₂O), so they migrate through the electrolyte from anode to cathode; this completes the circuit and maintains charge balance as electrons flow externally.
<!-- id: chem34a1-fc-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Green hydrogen and sustainable energy

**A413.** C. green hydrogen.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ghs-1 -->

Explanation: Green hydrogen comes from electrolysis powered by renewable energy. Grey hydrogen is made from natural gas by steam methane reforming without carbon capture; blue hydrogen uses the same process with carbon capture and storage.

**A414.** A. CH₄(g) + H₂O(g) → CO(g) + 3H₂(g)
<!-- id: chem34a1-ghs-2 -->

Explanation: Methane reacts with steam at high temperature over a nickel catalyst to form carbon monoxide and hydrogen; the CO is often converted to CO₂ by a further water–gas shift reaction, so the process emits CO₂.

**A415.** B. Use of renewable feedstocks and energy
<!-- id: chem34a1-ghs-3 -->

Explanation: Solar energy and water are renewable, replacing finite fossil fuels. The other principles relate to product breakdown, catalyst use and process monitoring.

**A416.** D. green hydrogen is currently more expensive to produce and needs large amounts of renewable electricity.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ghs-4 -->

Explanation: Hydrogen is identical whichever way it is made; the difference is the emissions from production. Electrolysers and renewable electricity currently make green hydrogen costlier than reforming natural gas.

**A417.** A. is a liquid under mild pressure and stores more hydrogen per litre than compressed hydrogen gas.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ghs-5 -->

Explanation: Ammonia liquefies at about −33 °C or under moderate pressure, making transport easier than hydrogen (boiling point −253 °C). Ammonia is toxic, so it is not 'safe' in that sense, and it contains no carbon.

**A418.** Water is electrolysed using electricity from renewable sources such as solar or wind; 2H₂O(l) → 2H₂(g) + O₂(g); the process uses renewable energy and an abundant feedstock (water), produces no CO₂, and using the hydrogen in a fuel cell regenerates water, so the cycle does not deplete resources or add greenhouse gases.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ghs-6 -->

Explanation:

**A419.** An energy source occurs naturally and can be harvested directly, e.g. solar energy from the Sun; an energy carrier stores and transports energy produced from a source, e.g. hydrogen must be manufactured (by electrolysis using solar power) and releases that energy later in a fuel cell.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ghs-7 -->

Explanation:

**A420.** Blue hydrogen lowers CO₂ emissions compared with grey hydrogen because much of the CO₂ is captured and stored underground; however, it still relies on a finite fossil fuel (natural gas), capture is not 100% efficient and methane leaks during extraction add greenhouse gases; therefore it is less sustainable than green hydrogen, though it may be a transitional option while renewable capacity grows.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ghs-8 -->

Explanation:

### Rates & equilibrium

#### Collision theory and reaction rates

**A421.** B. The particles must collide with energy at least equal to the activation energy and with the correct orientation
<!-- id: chem-3-1-1 -->

Explanation: A successful collision requires both sufficient energy (≥ Ea) to break bonds and an orientation that allows the reacting parts of the particles to come into contact.

**A422.** B. there are more acid particles per unit volume, so the frequency of collisions increases
<!-- id: chem-3-1-2 -->

Explanation: Increasing concentration increases the number of collisions per second; it does not change particle energies or the activation energy.

**A423.** D. has a larger surface area, increasing the frequency of collisions with acid particles
<!-- id: chem-3-1-3 -->

Explanation: Smaller particles expose more surface for collisions; the same mass gives the same amount of CO₂ and Ea is unchanged.

**A424.** The 2 M acid has more H⁺ ions per unit volume, so collisions between H⁺ and the CaCO₃ surface occur more frequently. With more collisions per second, there are more successful collisions per second (the proportion of successful collisions is unchanged), so the rate is greater.
<!-- id: chem-3-1-4 -->

Explanation: Full marks require linking concentration to collision frequency and to the number of successful collisions per unit time.

**A425.** D. the frequency of collisions between reactant particles increases
<!-- id: chem-3-1-5 -->

Explanation: Halving the volume doubles the concentration (partial pressure) of each gas, so particles collide more often.

**A426.** C. a much greater proportion of collisions have energy equal to or greater than the activation energy
<!-- id: chem-3-1-6 -->

Explanation: The fraction of particles with energy ≥ Ea increases sharply with temperature; the collision frequency increases only by a few per cent.

**A427.** A. Using larger lumps of zinc of the same total mass
<!-- id: chem-3-1-7 -->

Explanation: Larger lumps reduce the surface area exposed to the acid, which lowers the collision frequency and slows the reaction.

**A428.** At higher temperature particles have greater average kinetic energy, so collisions are slightly more frequent. More importantly, a much larger proportion of collisions now have energy equal to or greater than the activation energy, so the number of successful collisions per second increases greatly.
<!-- id: chem-3-1-8 -->

Explanation: Students must identify the increased proportion of collisions with E ≥ Ea as the dominant factor.

**A429.** B. The decrease in mass of the flask and contents over time
<!-- id: chem-3-1-9 -->

Explanation: CO₂ gas escapes from the open flask, so the mass decreases at a rate that reflects the reaction rate.

**A430.** B. the minimum energy colliding particles need for the collision to result in a reaction
<!-- id: chem-3-1-10 -->

Explanation: Ea is the energy barrier between reactants and the transition state that must be overcome in a collision.

**A431.** A. 170 kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-3-1-11 -->

Explanation: For an exothermic reaction, Ea(reverse) = Ea(forward) + |ΔH| = 50 + 120 = 170 kJ mol⁻¹.

**A432.** Ea(reverse) = Ea(forward) + |ΔH| = 185 + 92 = 277 kJ mol⁻¹.
<!-- id: chem-3-1-12 -->

Explanation: For an exothermic reaction the products sit lower than the reactants, so the reverse barrier is larger by |ΔH|.

**A433.** D. 50 kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-3-1-13 -->

Explanation: For an endothermic reaction the products are higher in energy, so Ea(reverse) = Ea(forward) − ΔH = 90 − 40 = 50 kJ mol⁻¹.

**A434.** B. the concentration of acid and the surface area of magnesium decrease, so collisions become less frequent
<!-- id: chem-3-1-14 -->

Explanation: As reactants are consumed, there are fewer reactant particles available to collide per unit time, so the rate falls.

**A435.** C. No change, because the concentrations of the reactant gases are unchanged
<!-- id: chem-3-1-15 -->

Explanation: At constant volume, adding an inert gas does not change the number of reactant particles per unit volume, so the frequency of reactant collisions is unchanged.

**A436.** n(CaCO₃) = 1.00 ÷ 100.1 = 9.99 × 10⁻³ mol = n(CO₂). Average rate = 9.99 × 10⁻³ ÷ 120 = 8.33 × 10⁻⁵ mol s⁻¹.
<!-- id: chem-3-1-16 -->

Explanation: CaCO₃ + 2HCl → CaCl₂ + H₂O + CO₂ gives a 1:1 mole ratio of CaCO₃ to CO₂.

**A437.** D. 45.9 mL
<!-- id: chem-3-1-17 -->

Explanation: n(Mg) = 0.0450 ÷ 24.3 = 1.85 × 10⁻³ mol = n(H₂); V = 1.85 × 10⁻³ × 24.8 = 0.0459 L = 45.9 mL.

**A438.** C. 1.33 mL s⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-3-1-18 -->

Explanation: Average rate = total volume ÷ total time = 80.0 mL ÷ 60 s = 1.33 mL s⁻¹.

**A439.** A. fewer collisions between reacting particles have energy equal to or greater than the activation energy
<!-- id: chem-3-1-19 -->

Explanation: Lowering temperature reduces particle kinetic energies, so a smaller fraction of collisions are successful (and collisions are also slightly less frequent).

**A440.** 1) Collect the hydrogen gas in a gas syringe (or inverted measuring cylinder) and record its volume at regular time intervals. 2) Measure the pH (or conductivity) of the solution over time as H⁺ is consumed, or time how long the magnesium takes to disappear completely.
<!-- id: chem-3-1-20 -->

Explanation: Any two valid methods that track a reactant or product over time are acceptable; mass loss is possible but small because H₂ is very light.

**A441.** A. has an extremely large surface area in contact with oxygen
<!-- id: chem-3-1-21 -->

Explanation: Fine dispersion exposes an enormous surface area to oxygen, so the collision frequency and hence rate of combustion become extremely high.

**A442.** C. Increasing the temperature
<!-- id: chem-3-1-22 -->

Explanation: Higher temperature makes particles move faster (more collisions) and gives more of them energy ≥ Ea; concentration and surface area only affect frequency.

**A443.** A. the particles collided with an orientation that did not allow the required bonds to break and form
<!-- id: chem-3-1-23 -->

Explanation: Even energetic collisions are unsuccessful if the reacting atoms are not aligned correctly at the moment of collision.

**A444.** Initially reactant concentrations are highest, so collision frequency and rate are greatest (steep slope). As magnesium and acid are used up, collisions become less frequent and the rate falls (flatter slope). The line becomes horizontal when the limiting reactant is fully consumed and no more gas is produced.
<!-- id: chem-3-1-24 -->

Explanation: The gradient at any point represents the rate of reaction.

**A445.** Heating does not change the activation energy. It increases the average kinetic energy of particles, so collisions are more frequent and a greater proportion of collisions have energy equal to or greater than the (unchanged) activation energy.
<!-- id: chem-3-1-25 -->

Explanation: Only a catalyst lowers the activation energy, by providing an alternative pathway.

**A446.** A. collide with energy equal to or greater than the activation energy and with the correct orientation.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ctr-1 -->

Explanation: Collisions must be both sufficiently energetic (≥ Ea) and correctly oriented to break bonds and form new ones; most collisions do not meet both conditions.

**A447.** B. Using a larger volume of the same concentration of acid
<!-- id: chem34a1-ctr-2 -->

Explanation: Rate depends on concentration, not total volume; a larger volume of the same acid does not change the frequency of collisions per unit area. Powdering increases surface area; higher concentration and temperature increase collision frequency and energy.

**A448.** D. a much greater proportion of collisions have energy equal to or greater than the activation energy.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ctr-3 -->

Explanation: Higher temperature slightly increases collision frequency, but the major effect is that a significantly larger fraction of particles have energy ≥ Ea. Temperature does not change Ea.

**A449.** A. is greatest at the start and decreases to zero as reactants are used up.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ctr-4 -->

Explanation: The rate is greatest initially when reactant concentration is highest; as HCl is consumed and CaCO₃ surface shrinks, the rate decreases until the limiting reactant is used and the curve levels off.

**A450.** C. the concentration of gas particles increases, so collisions occur more frequently.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ctr-5 -->

Explanation: Compressing a gas increases the number of particles per unit volume, so collision frequency increases. Ea is unchanged.

**A451.** Coal dust has a much larger surface area exposed to oxygen than a lump of the same mass; this greatly increases the frequency of collisions between carbon and O₂ molecules, so the reaction rate is so high that energy is released almost instantly, producing an explosion.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ctr-6 -->

Explanation:

**A452.** Experiment 2 has a steeper initial gradient (faster rate) because the higher HCl concentration increases collision frequency; it levels off sooner; it reaches the same final volume of H₂ because magnesium is the limiting reactant and the same amount is used in both.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ctr-7 -->

Explanation:

**A453.** Spoilage involves chemical (enzyme-catalysed and microbial) reactions; at lower temperature particles have less kinetic energy, so fewer collisions have energy ≥ activation energy and collisions are less frequent, so the rate of spoilage reactions decreases.
<!-- id: chem34a1-ctr-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Maxwell–Boltzmann distributions and catalysts

**A454.** B. the total number of particles in the sample
<!-- id: chem-3-2-1 -->

Explanation: The curve plots the number of particles at each kinetic energy, so the area under it equals the total number of particles.

**A455.** C. flattens and broadens, with the peak moving to higher energy and the area unchanged
<!-- id: chem-3-2-2 -->

Explanation: At higher temperature more particles have high kinetic energies; the number of particles (area) is unchanged, so the peak must be lower.

**A456.** A. the number of particles with enough energy to react on collision
<!-- id: chem-3-2-3 -->

Explanation: Only particles with kinetic energy ≥ Ea can react when they collide with correct orientation.

**A457.** The curve becomes flatter and broader, the peak shifts to a higher kinetic energy and is lower, and the total area stays the same. A greater area now lies beyond the activation energy, so more particles have energy ≥ Ea; more collisions are successful per second and the rate increases.
<!-- id: chem-3-2-4 -->

Explanation: Award marks for: peak shift right and lower, same area, larger area beyond Ea, link to more successful collisions.

**A458.** C. The curve is unchanged, but the activation energy is lower, so a larger area lies beyond it
<!-- id: chem-3-2-5 -->

Explanation: A catalyst does not change the energy distribution of particles; it provides a lower-Ea pathway, so more particles have sufficient energy.

**A459.** D. providing an alternative reaction pathway with a lower activation energy
<!-- id: chem-3-2-6 -->

Explanation: The catalysed pathway has a lower energy barrier, so a greater proportion of collisions are successful.

**A460.** C. ΔH is unchanged
<!-- id: chem-3-2-7 -->

Explanation: The reactants and products have the same energies with or without the catalyst; only the activation energy changes.

**A461.** The catalyst provides an alternative pathway with a lower activation energy. The distribution curve itself is unchanged, but the Ea line moves to lower energy, so a larger proportion of particles have energy ≥ Ea and the frequency of successful collisions increases.
<!-- id: chem-3-2-8 -->

Explanation: A common error is to say the curve shifts; for a catalyst only the position of Ea changes.

**A462.** C. The rates of the forward and reverse reactions increase equally and the equilibrium position does not change
<!-- id: chem-3-2-9 -->

Explanation: A catalyst lowers the activation energies of both forward and reverse reactions, so it does not alter the equilibrium position or K.

**A463.** A. heterogeneous catalysts, because they are solids and the reactants are gases
<!-- id: chem-3-2-10 -->

Explanation: A heterogeneous catalyst is in a different phase from the reactants; the gases adsorb onto the solid metal surface.

**A464.** B. the enzyme denatures, changing the shape of its active site
<!-- id: chem-3-2-11 -->

Explanation: High temperatures disrupt the bonds that maintain the enzyme's tertiary structure, so substrates no longer fit the active site.

**A465.** A homogeneous catalyst is in the same phase as the reactants, e.g. concentrated H₂SO₄ in the esterification of ethanoic acid and ethanol. A heterogeneous catalyst is in a different phase from the reactants, e.g. solid iron catalysing the gaseous Haber process (or Pt/Pd/Rh in a catalytic converter).
<!-- id: chem-3-2-12 -->

Explanation: Both a correct definition and a correct example are needed for each type.

**A466.** B. The total area under the curve
<!-- id: chem-3-2-13 -->

Explanation: The area represents the number of particles, which is constant for a fixed amount of gas.

**A467.** C. a taller peak at a lower kinetic energy
<!-- id: chem-3-2-14 -->

Explanation: At lower temperature particle energies are concentrated around a lower value, giving a taller, narrower peak with the same area.

**A468.** A. increases, because the catalyst also lowers the activation energy of the reverse reaction
<!-- id: chem-3-2-15 -->

Explanation: The catalysed pathway is shared by both directions, so both Ea values are lowered by the same amount.

**A469.** A catalyst lowers the activation energy of both the forward and reverse reactions by the same amount, so both rates increase equally. Equilibrium is reached sooner, but the equilibrium position and the value of K are unchanged, so the yield is the same.
<!-- id: chem-3-2-16 -->

Explanation: The key idea is that both directions are sped up equally.

**A470.** B. Concentrated sulfuric acid in the liquid-phase reaction between ethanoic acid and ethanol
<!-- id: chem-3-2-17 -->

Explanation: In esterification the H₂SO₄ catalyst is in the same (liquid) phase as the reactants; the others are solids catalysing gas reactions.

**A471.** D. Reactant molecules adsorb onto the catalyst surface, weakening their bonds and allowing reaction at lower energy
<!-- id: chem-3-2-18 -->

Explanation: Adsorption onto active sites weakens bonds and brings reactants together, then products desorb and the catalyst is regenerated.

**A472.** B. a larger surface area provides more active sites for reactant molecules to adsorb onto
<!-- id: chem-3-2-19 -->

Explanation: Heterogeneous catalysts act at their surfaces, so increasing surface area increases the catalysed rate.

**A473.** The poison binds strongly to the active sites on the metal catalyst surface and is not released. This blocks reactant molecules from adsorbing, so the catalyst's effectiveness decreases permanently.
<!-- id: chem-3-2-20 -->

Explanation: Catalyst poisoning reduces the number of available active sites.

**A474.** D. It has a lower peak, but the reactants and products are at the same energy levels
<!-- id: chem-3-2-21 -->

Explanation: A catalyst lowers only the activation energy; ΔH is unchanged so reactant and product levels stay the same.

**A475.** A. 80 kJ mol⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-3-2-22 -->

Explanation: Ea(reverse, catalysed) = Ea(forward, catalysed) + |ΔH| = 50 + 30 = 80 kJ mol⁻¹.

**A476.** D. a large relative increase in the number of particles in the high-energy tail beyond the activation energy
<!-- id: chem-3-2-23 -->

Explanation: Because Ea lies in the tail of the distribution, a small shift in the curve greatly increases the area beyond Ea.

**A477.** Ea(reverse, catalysed) = 140 + 196 = 336 kJ mol⁻¹.
<!-- id: chem-3-2-24 -->

Explanation: For an exothermic reaction the reverse barrier equals the forward barrier plus |ΔH|.

**A478.** Very few particles have zero (or near-zero) kinetic energy, so the curve begins at the origin. A small number of particles have very high energies, and there is no fixed maximum energy, so the curve tails off towards the axis without reaching it.
<!-- id: chem-3-2-25 -->

Explanation: The asymmetric shape reflects random collisions transferring energy between particles.

**A479.** C. the number of particles with energy equal to or greater than the activation energy.
<!-- id: chem34a1-mbc-1 -->

Explanation: The total area represents all particles; the area beyond Ea is the fraction with enough energy to react on collision.

**A480.** B. flattens and broadens, with the peak shifting to higher energy, while the total area stays the same.
<!-- id: chem34a1-mbc-2 -->

Explanation: At higher temperature the average kinetic energy increases and the energies spread out; the number of particles is unchanged, so the area under the curve is constant.

**A481.** D. providing an alternative reaction pathway with a lower activation energy.
<!-- id: chem34a1-mbc-3 -->

Explanation: A catalyst lowers Ea by providing a different mechanism, so on the Maxwell–Boltzmann curve the Ea line moves left and more particles have sufficient energy. The curve itself and ΔH are unchanged.

**A482.** C. It increases the rates of both forward and reverse reactions by lowering the activation energy of each.
<!-- id: chem34a1-mbc-4 -->

Explanation: The catalysed pathway is shared by both directions, so Ea is lowered for forward and reverse reactions equally (by the same amount). A catalyst is regenerated and does not change ΔH.

**A483.** A. heterogeneous catalysts that provide a surface where gas molecules adsorb and react.
<!-- id: chem34a1-mbc-5 -->

Explanation: The solid metals are in a different phase from the gaseous reactants, so they are heterogeneous catalysts; gases adsorb onto the surface, bonds weaken, and products desorb.

**A484.** Axes: number of particles (vertical) against kinetic energy (horizontal); curve starts at origin, rises to a peak and has a long tail that approaches but never reaches zero; the T₂ curve is lower and broader with its peak shifted to the right, with the same area; with Ea marked, the area to the right of Ea is much larger at T₂, so a greater proportion of collisions are successful and the rate increases.
<!-- id: chem34a1-mbc-6 -->

Explanation:

**A485.** A catalyst lowers the activation energy, so the Ea line moves to the left and the area under the curve beyond Ea increases, meaning more particles can react; the curve depends only on temperature (the energy distribution of the particles), which a catalyst does not change.
<!-- id: chem34a1-mbc-7 -->

Explanation:

**A486.** MnO₂ is a catalyst that provides an alternative pathway with lower activation energy, so more collisions are successful and O₂ is produced rapidly (bubbling); because it is regenerated at the end of the reaction, its mass is unchanged; it is a heterogeneous catalyst (solid catalyst with aqueous reactant).
<!-- id: chem34a1-mbc-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Equilibrium constants and calculations

**A487.** B. Kc = [NH₃]² / ([N₂][H₂]³)
<!-- id: chem-3-3-1 -->

Explanation: Kc = products over reactants, each concentration raised to the power of its coefficient in the balanced equation.

**A488.** B. M⁻²
<!-- id: chem-3-3-2 -->

Explanation: Units = M² ÷ (M × M³) = M⁻².

**A489.** C. 1/K
<!-- id: chem-3-3-3 -->

Explanation: Reversing an equation inverts the equilibrium expression, so the new constant is the reciprocal.

**A490.** Kc = [NO₂]² / ([NO]²[O₂]); units M⁻¹.
<!-- id: chem-3-3-4 -->

Explanation: Units: M² ÷ (M² × M) = M⁻¹.

**A491.** D. √K
<!-- id: chem-3-3-5 -->

Explanation: Halving all coefficients halves every power in the expression, so the new constant is K^(1/2).

**A492.** C. At equilibrium the mixture consists almost entirely of products
<!-- id: chem-3-3-6 -->

Explanation: A very large K means the equilibrium position lies far to the right; K says nothing about rate.

**A493.** B. 49.0
<!-- id: chem-3-3-7 -->

Explanation: Kc = [HI]² / ([H₂][I₂]) = 0.490 ÷ 0.0100 = 49.0.

**A494.** Initial [H₂] = [I₂] = 0.100 M. (2x)² ÷ (0.100 − x)² = 64.0, so 2x ÷ (0.100 − x) = 8.00, giving 10x = 0.800 and x = 0.0800. [HI] = 2x = 0.160 M.
<!-- id: chem-3-3-8 -->

Explanation: Check: (0.160)² ÷ (0.0200)² = 0.0256 ÷ 0.000400 = 64.0.

**A495.** A. The net reaction proceeds in the forward direction until Q = K
<!-- id: chem-3-3-9 -->

Explanation: Q < K means the ratio of products to reactants is too small, so more products form.

**A496.** D. Q = 8.0 × 10⁻⁴, so the net reaction proceeds forward, producing more NO₂
<!-- id: chem-3-3-10 -->

Explanation: Q = (0.020)² ÷ 0.50 = 8.0 × 10⁻⁴, which is less than Kc, so the forward reaction is favoured.

**A497.** D. Changing the temperature
<!-- id: chem-3-3-11 -->

Explanation: K depends only on temperature for a given equation; other changes shift the position but leave K unchanged.

**A498.** Q = (0.50)² ÷ ((0.10)² × 0.050) = 0.25 ÷ 0.00050 = 500 M⁻¹. Q > Kc, so the mixture is not at equilibrium and the net reaction proceeds in the reverse direction (forming more SO₂ and O₂).
<!-- id: chem-3-3-12 -->

Explanation: When Q > K there are too many products relative to reactants.

**A499.** A. It decreases
<!-- id: chem-3-3-13 -->

Explanation: Raising the temperature favours the endothermic (reverse) direction, lowering the product-to-reactant ratio and therefore Kc.

**A500.** A. 0.10 M
<!-- id: chem-3-3-14 -->

Explanation: At equilibrium [PCl₅] = 1.60 M and [PCl₃] = [Cl₂] = 0.40 M; Kc = (0.40 × 0.40) ÷ 1.60 = 0.10 M.

**A501.** D. 0.0579 M⁻²
<!-- id: chem-3-3-15 -->

Explanation: At equilibrium n(N₂) = 0.800, n(H₂) = 2.40, n(NH₃) = 0.400 mol, so concentrations are 0.400, 1.20 and 0.200 M; Kc = 0.0400 ÷ (0.400 × 1.728) = 0.0579 M⁻².

**A502.** Adding reactant makes Q smaller than K, so the forward reaction is temporarily favoured. Concentrations then adjust until the ratio of products to reactants again equals K. K depends only on temperature, which has not changed.
<!-- id: chem-3-3-16 -->

Explanation: Distinguish between the equilibrium position (which shifts) and the equilibrium constant (which does not).

**A503.** A. 0.80 M
<!-- id: chem-3-3-17 -->

Explanation: Kc = x ÷ (1.00 − x) = 4.0, so x = 4.0 − 4.0x, giving 5.0x = 4.0 and x = 0.80 M.

**A504.** C. 1.6 × 10⁵ M²
<!-- id: chem-3-3-18 -->

Explanation: Reversing gives 1/K = 400; doubling the coefficients squares this: 400² = 1.6 × 10⁵ M².

**A505.** C. No units
<!-- id: chem-3-3-19 -->

Explanation: There are two concentration terms on top and two on the bottom, so the units cancel.

**A506.** Reversing gives 1/2.0 = 0.50 M; doubling the coefficients squares this: (0.50)² = 0.25 M².
<!-- id: chem-3-3-20 -->

Explanation: Units: [A]²[B]² ÷ [C]² = M⁴ ÷ M² = M².

**A507.** D. The rates of the forward and reverse reactions are equal and non-zero
<!-- id: chem-3-3-21 -->

Explanation: At dynamic equilibrium both reactions continue at equal rates, so concentrations stay constant (but are not necessarily equal).

**A508.** B. 217 M⁻¹
<!-- id: chem-3-3-22 -->

Explanation: Reversing the equation gives the reciprocal: 1 ÷ (4.6 × 10⁻³) = 217 M⁻¹.

**A509.** C. PCl₅(g) ⇌ PCl₃(g) + Cl₂(g)
<!-- id: chem-3-3-23 -->

Explanation: For PCl₅ decomposition, units = (M × M) ÷ M = M; the others give no units, M⁻¹ and M⁻² respectively.

**A510.** In a closed system, the forward and reverse reactions continue to occur at equal rates, so there is no net change in the concentrations of reactants and products, even though particles are still reacting.
<!-- id: chem-3-3-24 -->

Explanation: Both 'equal rates' and 'reactions still occurring' must be stated.

**A511.** NO₂ reacted = 2 × 0.200 = 0.400 mol, so 0.400 mol NO₂ remains. [NO₂] = 0.200 M and [N₂O₄] = 0.100 M. Kc = 0.100 ÷ (0.200)² = 2.50 M⁻¹.
<!-- id: chem-3-3-25 -->

Explanation: Remember to use the 2:1 mole ratio and convert moles to concentrations before substituting.

**A512.** B. [NH₃]²/([N₂][H₂]³)
<!-- id: chem34a2-eq-1 -->

Explanation: K is products over reactants, each concentration raised to the power of its coefficient in the balanced equation: [NH₃]²/([N₂][H₂]³). The inverse expression is K for the reverse reaction, and coefficients are never used as multipliers or sums.

**A513.** D. M⁻¹ (L mol⁻¹)
<!-- id: chem34a2-eq-2 -->

Explanation: K = [SO₃]²/([SO₂]²[O₂]), so the units are M²/(M² × M) = M⁻¹, i.e. L mol⁻¹. K only has no units when total moles of gas are equal on both sides.

**A514.** A. 0.0625
<!-- id: chem34a2-eq-3 -->

Explanation: Reversing the equation inverts K (1/4.0 = 0.25), and doubling the coefficients squares it: (0.25)² = 0.0625. 0.25 only accounts for the reversal; 16 only accounts for doubling.

**A515.** C. The net reaction will proceed in the forward direction until Q increases to K.
<!-- id: chem34a2-eq-4 -->

Explanation: Q < K means there are relatively too few products, so the forward reaction is favoured until Q rises to equal K. K is fixed at a given temperature; only Q changes.

**A516.** B. At equilibrium, the mixture contains mainly products.
<!-- id: chem34a2-eq-5 -->

Explanation: A very large K means the equilibrium position lies far to the right, so products dominate. K gives no information about rate (kinetics) or the sign of ΔH.

**A517.** n(H₂) reacted = n(I₂) reacted = 0.780/2 = 0.390 mol, so 0.110 mol of each remains; equilibrium concentrations: [H₂] = [I₂] = 0.110/2.00 = 0.0550 M, [HI] = 0.780/2.00 = 0.390 M; K = [HI]²/([H₂][I₂]) = 0.390²/(0.0550 × 0.0550) = 50.3 (no units, as the mole totals on each side are equal).
<!-- id: chem34a2-eq-6 -->

Explanation:

**A518.** Q = [N₂O₄]/[NO₂]² = 0.50/(0.20)² = 12.5 M⁻¹; Q > K (12.5 > 6.0), so the system is not at equilibrium; net reverse reaction occurs, so [NO₂] increases (and [N₂O₄] decreases) until Q = K.
<!-- id: chem34a2-eq-7 -->

Explanation:

**A519.** In a closed system the forward and reverse reactions continue to occur at equal rates, so the concentrations of reactants and products stay constant (no net change), even though particles are still reacting; only a change in temperature changes the value of K.
<!-- id: chem34a2-eq-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Le Chatelier's principle and yield optimisation

**A520.** D. The equilibrium shifts to the right, because there are fewer gas particles on the product side
<!-- id: chem-3-4-1 -->

Explanation: Increasing pressure favours the side with fewer moles of gas (2 vs 4), partially opposing the increase.

**A521.** C. The rate increases but the equilibrium yield of ammonia decreases
<!-- id: chem-3-4-2 -->

Explanation: Higher temperature speeds up both reactions but favours the endothermic reverse reaction, reducing the yield.

**A522.** D. a moderate temperature gives an acceptable yield at a fast enough rate (a compromise)
<!-- id: chem-3-4-3 -->

Explanation: At low temperatures the rate is uneconomically slow; ~450 °C balances rate against yield.

**A523.** Increasing pressure (by decreasing volume) increases the concentration of all gases. The system partially opposes this by shifting to the side with fewer gas particles; there are 4 mol of gas on the left and 2 mol on the right, so the equilibrium shifts right, increasing the yield of ammonia.
<!-- id: chem-3-4-4 -->

Explanation: Reference to the mole ratio of gas particles (4:2) is required.

**A524.** C. It does not change
<!-- id: chem-3-4-5 -->

Explanation: A catalyst increases forward and reverse rates equally, so the equilibrium position is unaffected.

**A525.** D. It does not shift
<!-- id: chem-3-4-6 -->

Explanation: At constant volume the concentrations (partial pressures) of the reacting gases are unchanged, so Q still equals K.

**A526.** D. shift the equilibrium to the right, increasing the overall conversion of reactants
<!-- id: chem-3-4-7 -->

Explanation: Removing a product lowers Q below K, so the forward reaction is favoured to replace it.

**A527.** Because the reaction is exothermic, lower temperatures give a higher equilibrium yield. However, at low temperatures the rate is too slow to be economical. About 450 °C (with an iron catalyst) is a compromise that gives an acceptable yield at a fast enough rate.
<!-- id: chem-3-4-8 -->

Explanation: Both the yield argument and the rate argument must be addressed.

**A528.** A. The solution becomes a deeper red
<!-- id: chem-3-4-9 -->

Explanation: Adding SCN⁻ shifts the equilibrium right, forming more red FeSCN²⁺.

**A529.** A. The red colour becomes paler
<!-- id: chem-3-4-10 -->

Explanation: Removing SCN⁻ as a precipitate shifts the equilibrium left, reducing the concentration of FeSCN²⁺.

**A530.** C. The mixture becomes darker brown
<!-- id: chem-3-4-11 -->

Explanation: Heating favours the endothermic reverse reaction, producing more brown NO₂.

**A531.** The colour immediately becomes darker because [NO₂] increases when the volume decreases. The system then shifts right, towards fewer gas particles, converting some NO₂ to N₂O₄, so the colour lightens. The final colour is still darker than the original because [NO₂] is only partially restored.
<!-- id: chem-3-4-12 -->

Explanation: Both the immediate effect and the subsequent shift must be explained.

**A532.** D. The colour darkens immediately, then lightens somewhat but remains darker than originally
<!-- id: chem-3-4-13 -->

Explanation: Compression instantly doubles [NO₂]; the system then shifts right (fewer gas particles), partially but not fully reducing [NO₂].

**A533.** B. No shift occurs, because there are equal numbers of gas particles on each side
<!-- id: chem-3-4-14 -->

Explanation: With 2 mol of gas on each side, changing volume changes Q and K by the same factor, so there is no net shift.

**A534.** A. Low temperature and high pressure
<!-- id: chem-3-4-15 -->

Explanation: The forward reaction is exothermic and reduces the number of gas particles (3 → 2).

**A535.** Kc increases and the yield of hydrogen increases. The forward reaction is endothermic, so increasing temperature favours the forward direction to partially absorb the added heat, increasing the product concentrations relative to reactants.
<!-- id: chem-3-4-16 -->

Explanation: Temperature is the only factor that changes K.

**A536.** C. the conversion to SO₃ is already very high at low pressure, so the extra cost of high pressure is not justified
<!-- id: chem-3-4-17 -->

Explanation: Conversions of about 99% are achieved at modest pressure, so expensive high-pressure equipment is unnecessary.

**A537.** A. It shifts to the left, the side with more dissolved particles
<!-- id: chem-3-4-18 -->

Explanation: Dilution lowers the total concentration of particles; the system partially opposes this by favouring the side with more particles (Q > K after dilution).

**A538.** A. It is lower
<!-- id: chem-3-4-19 -->

Explanation: The added H₂ shifts the equilibrium right, consuming some N₂ to form NH₃.

**A539.** A catalyst speeds up the forward and reverse reactions equally, so the equilibrium position and yield are unchanged. However, equilibrium is reached much faster, and a reasonable rate can be achieved at a lower temperature, where the equilibrium yield is higher, saving time and energy costs.
<!-- id: chem-3-4-20 -->

Explanation: Award a mark for the rate benefit and one for enabling lower operating temperature.

**A540.** B. High temperature and low pressure
<!-- id: chem-3-4-21 -->

Explanation: The forward reaction is endothermic and increases the number of gas particles (2 → 4).

**A541.** B. If an equilibrium system is subjected to a change, the system adjusts to partially oppose the effect of that change
<!-- id: chem-3-4-22 -->

Explanation: The system shifts to partially counteract the change; it cannot fully restore the original conditions.

**A542.** B. The value of K has increased and the yield of products is greater
<!-- id: chem-3-4-23 -->

Explanation: Raising temperature favours the endothermic forward direction, increasing both the product concentrations and K.

**A543.** 1) Use an excess of ethanol (or ethanoic acid): increasing a reactant concentration shifts the equilibrium right. 2) Remove a product as it forms, e.g. by distilling off the ester or using concentrated H₂SO₄ to absorb water: lowering product concentration shifts the equilibrium right.
<!-- id: chem-3-4-24 -->

Explanation: Any two valid methods with a Le Chatelier explanation are acceptable; a catalyst alone does not increase yield.

**A544.** [O₂] is higher than originally (but lower than immediately after the addition); [SO₂] is lower; [SO₃] is higher; Kc is unchanged because the temperature is constant.
<!-- id: chem-3-4-25 -->

Explanation: The system only partially opposes the addition of O₂, so [O₂] remains above its original value.

**A545.** B. The mixture becomes browner and the value of K increases.
<!-- id: chem34a2-lc-1 -->

Explanation: Increasing temperature favours the endothermic (forward) direction to partially absorb the added energy, producing more brown NO₂. For an endothermic reaction, K increases with temperature, so K does change.

**A546.** D. The forward and reverse rates increase equally, so the position does not change.
<!-- id: chem34a2-lc-2 -->

Explanation: A catalyst lowers the activation energy for both the forward and reverse reactions by the same amount, so both rates increase equally. Equilibrium is reached faster, but the position and K are unchanged.

**A547.** A. The colour becomes darker and the equilibrium position does not shift.
<!-- id: chem34a2-lc-3 -->

Explanation: Halving the volume doubles every concentration, so [I₂] increases and the colour darkens. Both sides have 2 mol of gas, so Q still equals K and there is no shift.

**A548.** C. The equilibrium position does not change.
<!-- id: chem34a2-lc-4 -->

Explanation: At constant volume, adding an inert gas does not change the concentrations (partial pressures) of the reacting species, so Q stays equal to K and nothing shifts. A total pressure rise alone is not a disturbance to the equilibrium.

**A549.** B. Some SO₃ was added to the vessel.
<!-- id: chem34a2-lc-5 -->

Explanation: Only [SO₃] shows an instantaneous jump, which indicates SO₃ was added. A volume change would make all concentrations jump at once; a temperature change produces only gradual changes; a catalyst causes no change at all once at equilibrium.

**A550.** Decreasing volume increases the concentrations of all gases (increases pressure); by Le Chatelier's principle the system partially opposes this by shifting to the side with fewer gas particles, i.e. forward (3 mol → 1 mol); so the yield of methanol increases; K is unchanged because temperature is constant.
<!-- id: chem34a2-lc-6 -->

Explanation:

**A551.** Dilution halves every concentration, so Q = [FeSCN²⁺]/([Fe³⁺][SCN⁻]) becomes (½)/(½ × ½) = 2 × the original value, i.e. Q > K; the net reverse reaction occurs (towards the side with more particles in solution) until Q = K; the solution becomes paler red, both due to dilution and to some FeSCN²⁺ converting back to Fe³⁺ and SCN⁻.
<!-- id: chem34a2-lc-7 -->

Explanation:

**A552.** There is no sudden vertical jump in any concentration at the moment of change; [NH₃] gradually decreases while [N₂] and [H₂] gradually increase (the changes in N₂ : H₂ : NH₃ are in the ratio 1 : 3 : 2) until new constant (horizontal) values are reached; K decreases because the reverse (endothermic) reaction is favoured at higher temperature.
<!-- id: chem34a2-lc-8 -->

Explanation:

**A553.** C. Lower temperatures would give a higher yield, but the rate would be too slow to be economical.
<!-- id: chem34a2-ind-1 -->

Explanation: Because the forward reaction is exothermic, lower temperatures favour a higher equilibrium yield. However, fewer particles would have energy ≥ Ea, so the rate would be uneconomically slow; ~400–450 °C is a compromise between rate and yield.

**A554.** A. It increases the rate of reaction without changing the equilibrium yield.
<!-- id: chem34a2-ind-2 -->

Explanation: A catalyst provides an alternative pathway with lower activation energy, so equilibrium is reached faster. It does not alter ΔH, K or the equilibrium yield.

**A555.** B. It increases the overall conversion of N₂ and H₂ into ammonia.
<!-- id: chem34a2-ind-3 -->

Explanation: Removing NH₃ and recycling unreacted gases means reactants pass through the reactor repeatedly, so nearly all N₂ and H₂ are eventually converted. K depends only on temperature and is not changed.

**A556.** D. Conversion is already very high (about 99%) at low pressure, so the extra cost of high-pressure equipment is not justified.
<!-- id: chem34a2-ind-4 -->

Explanation: Le Chatelier's principle predicts higher pressure would increase yield, but with a V₂O₅ catalyst, moderate temperature and multiple catalyst beds the conversion is already near-complete, so the cost and safety risks of high pressure are not worthwhile.

**A557.** C. Rate increases; equilibrium yield decreases.
<!-- id: chem34a2-ind-5 -->

Explanation: Higher temperature increases the proportion of successful collisions, so rate increases. For an exothermic forward reaction, higher temperature favours the reverse reaction, so K and the equilibrium yield decrease.

**A558.** Lower temperature would favour a higher equilibrium yield (exothermic forward reaction) but the rate would be too slow, so a moderate temperature with a catalyst gives an acceptable rate; high pressure increases both rate (more frequent collisions) and yield (shift to fewer gas particles, 3 mol → 1 mol); pressure is not raised further because of the energy cost of compression and the cost and safety risk of stronger equipment.
<!-- id: chem34a2-ind-6 -->

Explanation:

**A559.** n(N₂) = 10.0 × 10⁶ g / 28.0 g mol⁻¹ = 3.57 × 10⁵ mol; n(NH₃) theoretical = 2 × 3.57 × 10⁵ = 7.14 × 10⁵ mol, mass = 7.14 × 10⁵ × 17.0 = 1.21 × 10⁷ g (12.1 t); percentage yield = 2.50/12.1 × 100 = 20.6%.
<!-- id: chem34a2-ind-7 -->

Explanation:

**A560.** A catalyst lowers the activation energy, so an acceptable rate can be reached at a lower temperature; this means less energy (often from fossil fuels) is needed for heating, reducing costs and CO₂ emissions; a catalyst is also not consumed, so it can be reused.
<!-- id: chem34a2-ind-8 -->

Explanation:

### Electrolysis & rechargeable cells

#### Electrolytic cell design and products

**A561.** A. anode, which is connected to the positive terminal of the power supply
<!-- id: chem-4-1-1 -->

Explanation: Oxidation always occurs at the anode; in an electrolytic cell the anode is the positive electrode because the power supply draws electrons away from it.

**A562.** B. Electrical energy to chemical energy
<!-- id: chem-4-1-2 -->

Explanation: An external power supply forces a non-spontaneous reaction to occur, storing electrical energy as chemical energy in the products.

**A563.** C. sodium liquid
<!-- id: chem-4-1-3 -->

Explanation: With no water present, the only species that can be reduced is Na⁺, giving Na(l) at the cathode.

**A564.** Anode: 2Br⁻(l) → Br₂(g) + 2e⁻. Cathode: K⁺(l) + e⁻ → K(l).
<!-- id: chem-4-1-4 -->

Explanation: Only K⁺ and Br⁻ are present in the melt; states should be (l) for the ions in the melt.

**A565.** D. chlorine gas
<!-- id: chem-4-1-5 -->

Explanation: Cl⁻ is the only species available to be oxidised in the melt: 2Cl⁻ → Cl₂ + 2e⁻.

**A566.** A. Hydrogen gas and hydroxide ions
<!-- id: chem-4-1-6 -->

Explanation: Water (E° = −0.83 V) is a far stronger oxidant than Na⁺ (E° = −2.71 V), so 2H₂O + 2e⁻ → H₂ + 2OH⁻ occurs.

**A567.** B. 2 : 1
<!-- id: chem-4-1-7 -->

Explanation: Water is electrolysed: 2H₂O → 2H₂ + O₂, so hydrogen (cathode) and oxygen (anode) form in a 2 : 1 mole and volume ratio.

**A568.** Water is a stronger oxidant than Na⁺ (E° −0.83 V compared with −2.71 V), so water is reduced at the cathode to H₂ and OH⁻ instead of Na⁺ being reduced to Na.
<!-- id: chem-4-1-8 -->

Explanation: Refer to relative oxidant strengths using the electrochemical series.

**A569.** C. The blue colour fades and the solution becomes more acidic
<!-- id: chem-4-1-9 -->

Explanation: Cu²⁺ is removed as copper at the cathode (colour fades) while water is oxidised at the anode producing O₂ and H⁺ (pH falls).

**A570.** D. The anode is oxidised to Cu²⁺ and the concentration of Cu²⁺ in solution stays approximately constant
<!-- id: chem-4-1-10 -->

Explanation: Copper metal is a stronger reductant than water, so the anode dissolves at the same rate Cu²⁺ is deposited at the cathode.

**A571.** A. the high Cl⁻ concentration and the large overpotential for O₂ formation favour oxidation of Cl⁻
<!-- id: chem-4-1-11 -->

Explanation: Under standard conditions water (1.23 V) would be oxidised before Cl⁻ (1.36 V), but high [Cl⁻] and the overpotential for O₂ make Cl₂ the main product.

**A572.** 2H₂O(l) → 2H₂(g) + O₂(g)
<!-- id: chem-4-1-12 -->

Explanation: Cathode: 4H⁺ + 4e⁻ → 2H₂; anode: 2H₂O → O₂ + 4H⁺ + 4e⁻; H⁺ cancels, leaving decomposition of water.

**A573.** B. iodine and hydrogen
<!-- id: chem-4-1-13 -->

Explanation: I⁻ (E° 0.54 V) is a stronger reductant than water (1.23 V) so it is oxidised to I₂; water is reduced to H₂ in preference to K⁺.

**A574.** C. water would be reduced in preference to Al³⁺
<!-- id: chem-4-1-14 -->

Explanation: Al³⁺ (E° = −1.66 V) is a much weaker oxidant than water (−0.83 V), so in water only H₂ would form at the cathode.

**A575.** D. 0.89 V
<!-- id: chem-4-1-15 -->

Explanation: Minimum voltage = E°(anode reaction couple) − E°(cathode reaction couple) = 1.23 − 0.34 = 0.89 V.

**A576.** 2Ni²⁺(aq) + 2H₂O(l) → 2Ni(s) + O₂(g) + 4H⁺(aq)
<!-- id: chem-4-1-16 -->

Explanation: Ni²⁺ (−0.23 V) is a stronger oxidant than water (−0.83 V), so Ni deposits; sulfate is not oxidised so water is oxidised to O₂.

**A577.** A. from the anode to the positive terminal of the power supply, and from the negative terminal to the cathode
<!-- id: chem-4-1-17 -->

Explanation: Electrons are released at the anode, pumped by the power supply and delivered to the cathode; ions, not electrons, carry charge through the electrolyte.

**A578.** B. cathode, which is negative
<!-- id: chem-4-1-18 -->

Explanation: Positive ions are attracted to the negative electrode, which in an electrolytic cell is the cathode where reduction occurs.

**A579.** C. silver at the cathode and oxygen at the anode
<!-- id: chem-4-1-19 -->

Explanation: Ag⁺ (0.80 V) is a stronger oxidant than water so silver deposits; NO₃⁻ is not oxidised, so water is oxidised to O₂.

**A580.** 0.54 − (−0.83) = 1.37 V
<!-- id: chem-4-1-20 -->

Explanation: The applied voltage must at least equal the difference between the oxidation and reduction couples; in practice more is needed because of resistance and overpotential.

**A581.** D. increases
<!-- id: chem-4-1-21 -->

Explanation: Reduction of water at the cathode produces OH⁻ ions, raising the local pH.

**A582.** A. lower the melting point of the electrolyte and reduce energy costs
<!-- id: chem-4-1-22 -->

Explanation: The NaCl/CaCl₂ mixture melts at about 600 °C rather than 801 °C, saving energy.

**A583.** B. they would react together to re-form sodium chloride
<!-- id: chem-4-1-23 -->

Explanation: Sodium is a powerful reductant and chlorine a powerful oxidant; if they mix they react vigorously, reversing the electrolysis.

**A584.** The reaction in an electrolytic cell is non-spontaneous (the strongest oxidant present lies below the strongest reductant in the electrochemical series), so electrical energy must be supplied to force electrons from the anode to the cathode.
<!-- id: chem-4-1-24 -->

Explanation: Mention non-spontaneity and that the supply drives electron flow.

**A585.** They are inert: they conduct electricity but do not react themselves, so the products depend only on the species in the electrolyte.
<!-- id: chem-4-1-25 -->

Explanation: A reactive anode, such as copper, would itself be oxidised and change the products.

**A586.** A. Oxidation occurs at the anode, which is the positive electrode.
<!-- id: chem34a2-ely-1 -->

Explanation: In all cells oxidation occurs at the anode. In an electrolytic cell the anode is connected to the positive terminal of the power supply, which draws electrons away from it. (In a galvanic cell the anode is negative.)

**A587.** B. Cathode: Na(l); anode: Cl₂(g)
<!-- id: chem34a2-ely-2 -->

Explanation: In the molten salt the only species are Na⁺ and Cl⁻ (no water). Na⁺ is reduced at the cathode: Na⁺(l) + e⁻ → Na(l); Cl⁻ is oxidised at the anode: 2Cl⁻(l) → Cl₂(g) + 2e⁻.

**A588.** D. H₂(g) at the cathode and O₂(g) at the anode, in a 2 : 1 volume ratio
<!-- id: chem34a2-ely-3 -->

Explanation: Water is a stronger oxidant than Na⁺ (−0.83 V vs −2.71 V), so 2H₂O + 2e⁻ → H₂ + 2OH⁻ at the cathode. SO₄²⁻ is not oxidised; water is: 2H₂O → O₂ + 4H⁺ + 4e⁻. Balancing electrons gives 2 H₂ : 1 O₂.

**A589.** C. The anode loses mass, the cathode gains mass, and [Cu²⁺] stays approximately constant.
<!-- id: chem34a2-ely-4 -->

Explanation: Cu(s) is a stronger reductant than water, so the copper anode is oxidised: Cu → Cu²⁺ + 2e⁻. Cu²⁺ is reduced at the cathode. Cu²⁺ enters and leaves at the same rate, so its concentration stays about constant (the basis of copper refining).

**A590.** B. An electrolytic cell uses electrical energy to drive a non-spontaneous redox reaction.
<!-- id: chem34a2-ely-5 -->

Explanation: An electrolytic cell converts electrical energy into chemical energy, forcing a reaction that would not occur spontaneously. A galvanic cell does the reverse. Electrolytic cells often have a single electrolyte container with no salt bridge, and their cathode is negative while a galvanic cathode is positive.

**A591.** Cathode: 2H₂O(l) + 2e⁻ → H₂(g) + 2OH⁻(aq) (water, −0.83 V, is a stronger oxidant than K⁺, −2.93 V); anode: 2I⁻(aq) → I₂(aq) + 2e⁻ (I⁻ is a stronger reductant than H₂O, since I₂/I⁻ at +0.54 V is below O₂/H₂O at +1.23 V); overall: 2I⁻(aq) + 2H₂O(l) → I₂(aq) + H₂(g) + 2OH⁻(aq); observations: bubbles of colourless gas at the cathode (pH rises there), brown/yellow colour forming around the anode.
<!-- id: chem34a2-ely-6 -->

Explanation:

**A592.** In aqueous solution water is a much stronger oxidant than Na⁺ (E° −0.83 V vs −2.71 V), so water would be reduced to H₂ at the cathode instead of Na⁺; in addition, any sodium formed would react vigorously with water; in molten NaCl there is no water, so Na⁺ is the only species that can be reduced.
<!-- id: chem34a2-ely-7 -->

Explanation:

**A593.** The spoon is the cathode, connected to the negative terminal; a silver anode is connected to the positive terminal, with AgNO₃(aq) (a solution of Ag⁺) as the electrolyte; cathode: Ag⁺(aq) + e⁻ → Ag(s); anode: Ag(s) → Ag⁺(aq) + e⁻, which replaces the Ag⁺ removed so [Ag⁺] stays constant.
<!-- id: chem34a2-ely-8 -->

Explanation:

#### Faraday's laws and calculations

**A594.** A. 1.20 × 10³ C
<!-- id: chem-4-2-1 -->

Explanation: Q = It = 2.00 × 600 = 1200 C; forgetting to convert minutes to seconds gives 20.0 C.

**A595.** B. 0.100 mol
<!-- id: chem-4-2-2 -->

Explanation: n(e⁻) = Q/F = 9650/96 500 = 0.100 mol.

**A596.** C. 6.35 g
<!-- id: chem-4-2-3 -->

Explanation: Cu²⁺ + 2e⁻ → Cu, so n(Cu) = 0.100 mol and m = 0.100 × 63.5 = 6.35 g.

**A597.** Q = 1.50 × 2700 = 4050 C; n(e⁻) = 4050/96 500 = 0.04197 mol; n(Ni) = 0.02098 mol; m = 0.02098 × 58.7 = 1.23 g.
<!-- id: chem-4-2-4 -->

Explanation: Award marks for Q, mol e⁻, mole ratio 2:1 and final mass to 3 sig figs.

**A598.** D. 20.1 g
<!-- id: chem-4-2-5 -->
